# C.5 BENEFIT OF DEPTH: POLYNOMIAL VS EXPONENTIAL DEPENDENCE ON INITIALIZATION

To verify the our theory in Section 6, we run gradient descent with different depth and initialization. The results are shown in Figure 5. We can see that as the initialization becomes smaller, the final solution gets closer to the ground truth. However, a depth-2 model requires exponentially small initialization, while deeper models require polynomial small initialization, though it takes much longer to converge.

# D THE MARGINAL VALUE OF BEING DEeper

Theorem D.1 shows that the end-to-end dynamics (19) converges point-wise while  $L \to \infty$  if the product of learning rate and depth,  $\eta L$ , is fixed as constant. Interestingly, (19) also allows us to simulate the dynamics of  $W(t)$  for all depths  $L$  while the computation time is independent of  $L$ . In Figure 6, we compare the effect of depth while fixing the initialization and  $\eta L$ . We can see that deeper models converge faster. The difference between  $L = 1, 2$ , and 4 is large, while difference among  $L \geq 16$  is marginal.

![](images/1dcdd999c825958ef682fa617949f88cf1b328cf14534388c11e82ce61e33739.jpg)  
Figure 6: The marginal value of being deeper. The trajectory of GD converges when depth goes to infinity. Solid (dotted) curves correspond to test (train) loss. The  $x$ -axis stands for the normalized continuous time  $t$  (multiplied by  $L$ ).

Theorem D.1. Suppose  $\boldsymbol{W} = \tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}^{\top}$  is the SVD decomposition of  $\boldsymbol{W}$ , where  $\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}} = \mathrm{diag}(\sigma_1,\dots ,\sigma_d)$ . The dynamics of  $L$ -layer linear net is the following,  $\circ$  denotes the entry-wise multiplication:

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {W}}{\mathrm {d} t} = - L \tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} \left(\left(\tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {\top} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}\right) \circ \boldsymbol {K} ^ {(L)}\right) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} ^ {\top}, \tag {19}
$$

where  $K_{i,i}^{(L)} = \sigma_i^{2 - 2 / L}, K_{i,j}^{(L)} = \frac{\sigma_i^2 - \sigma_j^2}{L\sigma_i^{2 / L} - L\sigma_j^{2 / L}}$  for  $i \neq j$ .

Proof. We start from (11):

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {W}}{\mathrm {d} t} = - \sum_ {l = 0} ^ {L - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {W} \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {\frac {1}{L}} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \left(\boldsymbol {W} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {W}\right) ^ {\frac {L - 1 - l}{L}} \\ = - \sum_ {l = 0} ^ {L - 1} \tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} \tilde {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} ^ {\frac {2 l}{L}} \tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {\top} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} \tilde {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} ^ {\frac {2 (L - 1 - l)}{L}} \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} \\ = - L \tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} \left[ L ^ {- 1} \sum_ {l = 0} ^ {L - 1} \tilde {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} ^ {\frac {2 l}{L}} (\tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {\top} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} ^ {\frac {2 (L - 1 - l)}{L}} \right] \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Note that  $\tilde{\Sigma}$  is diagonal, so

$$
\tilde {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} ^ {\frac {2 l}{L}} (\tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {\top} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} ^ {\frac {2 (L - 1 - l)}{L}} = (\tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {\top} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}) \circ \boldsymbol {H} ^ {(l)},
$$

where  $\pmb{H}_{i,j}^{(l)} = \sigma_i^{\frac{2l}{L}}\sigma_j^{\frac{2(L - 1 - l)}{L}}$  . Therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} L ^ {- 1} \sum_ {l = 0} ^ {L - 1} \tilde {\Sigma} ^ {\frac {2 l}{L}} (\tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {\top} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}) \tilde {\Sigma} ^ {\frac {2 (L - 1 - l)}{L}} = L ^ {- 1} \sum_ {l = 0} ^ {L - 1} (\tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {\top} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}) \circ \boldsymbol {H} ^ {(l)} \\ = \left(\tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {\top} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}\right) \circ \boldsymbol {K} ^ {(L)}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\pmb{K}^{(L)} = L^{-1}\sum_{l = 0}^{L - 1}\pmb{H}^{(l)}$ . Hence,

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {W}}{\mathrm {d} t} = - L \tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} \left[ (\tilde {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {\top} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}) \circ \boldsymbol {K} ^ {(L)} \right] \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}}.
$$

The entries of  $\pmb{K}^{(L)}$  can be directly calculated by

$$
K _ {i, j} ^ {(L)} = L ^ {- 1} \sum_ {l = 0} ^ {L - 1} \sigma_ {i} ^ {\frac {2 l}{L}} \sigma_ {j} ^ {\frac {2 (L - 1 - l)}{L}} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \sigma_ {i} ^ {2 - 2 / L}, & i = j, \\ \frac {\sigma_ {i} ^ {2} - \sigma_ {j} ^ {2}}{L \sigma_ {i} ^ {2 / L} - L \sigma_ {j} ^ {2 / L}}, & i \neq j. \end{array} \right.
$$

![](images/9315b575d3ea733f6e395f18ed92cd9cddd03448d3e9b5bda1fe254cfafb2f05.jpg)

Corollary D.2. As  $L \to \infty$ ,  $\pmb{K}^{(L)}$  converges to  $\pmb{K}^*$ , where  $K_{i,i}^* = \sigma_i^2$ ,  $K_{i,j}^* = \frac{\sigma_i^2 - \sigma_j^2}{\ln\sigma_i^2 - \ln\sigma_j^2}$  for  $i \neq j$ .

Experiment details. We follow the general setting in Appendix C.1. The ground truth  $\mathbf{W}^*$  is different but is generated in the same manner and has the same shape of  $20 \times 20$  and  $p = 0.3$  is used for observation generation. We directly apply (19), in which we compute  $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}$  and  $\tilde{\mathbf{U}}$  through SVD, to simulate the trajectory together with a constant learning rate of  $\frac{10^{-3}}{L}$  for depth  $L$ .  $\mathbf{W}(0)$  is sampled from  $10^{-3} \times \mathcal{N}(0, I_d)$ .

# E PROOFS FOR DYNAMICAL SYSTEM

In this section, we prove Theorem 5.3 in Section 5.1. In Appendix E.1, we show how to reduce Theorem 5.3 to the case where  $J(\mathbf{0})$  is exactly a diagonal matrix, then we prove this diagonal case in Appendix E.2. Finally, in Appendix E.3, we discuss how to extend it to the case where  $J(\mathbf{0})$  is non-diagonalizable.

# E.1 REDUCTION TO THE DIAGONAL CASE

Theorem E.1. If  $J(\mathbf{0}) = \mathrm{diag}(\tilde{\mu}_1, \dots, \tilde{\mu}_d)$  is diagonal, then the statement in Theorem 5.3 holds.

Proof for Theorem 5.3. We show how to prove Theorem 5.3 based on Theorem E.1. Let  $\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{\mathrm{d}t} = g(\theta)$  be the dynamical system in Theorem 5.3. Let  $J(\mathbf{0}) = \tilde{V}\tilde{D}\tilde{V}^{-1}$  be the eigendecomposition, where  $\tilde{V}$  is an invertible matrix and  $\tilde{D} = \mathrm{diag}(\tilde{\mu}_1,\dots ,\tilde{\mu}_d)$ . Now we define the following new dynamics by changing the basis:

$$
\hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) = \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\theta} (t).
$$

Then  $\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t} = \hat{\pmb{g}} (\hat{\pmb{\theta}})$  for  $\hat{g} (\hat{\pmb{\theta}})\coloneqq \tilde{V}^{-1}\pmb {g}(\tilde{V}\hat{\pmb{\theta}})$ , and the associated Jacobian matrix is  $\hat{J} (\hat{\pmb{\theta}})\coloneqq \tilde{V}^{-1}\pmb {J}(\tilde{V}\hat{\pmb{\theta}})\tilde{V}$ , and thus  $\hat{J} (\mathbf{0}) = \mathrm{diag}(\tilde{\mu}_1,\dots ,\tilde{\mu}_d)$ .

Now we apply Theorem E.1 to  $\hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t)$ . Then  $\hat{\pmb{z}}_{\alpha}(t) \coloneqq \tilde{\pmb{V}}^{-1}\pmb{z}_{\alpha}(t)$  converges to the limit  $\hat{\pmb{z}}(t) \coloneqq \lim_{\alpha \to 0} \hat{\pmb{z}}_{\alpha}(t)$ . This shows that the limit  $\pmb{z}(t) = \tilde{\pmb{V}}\hat{\pmb{z}}(t)$  exists in Theorem 5.3. We can also verify that  $\pmb{z}(t)$  is a solution of (6).

Given  $\delta_{\alpha}$  converging to 0 with positive alignment with  $\tilde{\pmb{u}}_1$  as  $\alpha \to 0$ , we can define  $\hat{\delta}_{\alpha} \coloneqq \tilde{\pmb{V}}^{-1}\delta_{\alpha}$ , then  $\hat{\delta}_{\alpha}$  converges to 0 with positive alignment with  $e_1$ , where  $e_1$  is the first vector in the standard basis and is also the top eigenvector of  $\hat{J}(\mathbf{0})$ . Therefore, for every  $t \in (-\infty, +\infty)$ , there is a constant  $C > 0$  such that

$$
\left\| \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} ^ {- 1} \phi \left(\delta_ {\alpha}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\langle \delta_ {\alpha} , \tilde {\boldsymbol {u}} _ {1} \rangle}\right) - \hat {\boldsymbol {z}} (t) \right\| _ {2} \leq C \cdot \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\delta}} _ {\alpha} \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {\bar {\gamma}}{\bar {\mu} _ {1} + \bar {\gamma}}} \tag {20}
$$

for every sufficiently small  $\alpha$ . As  $\tilde{V}$  are invertible, this directly implies (7).

![](images/7b51771eedac96cd39ecf9e591590b4cee0f10531d8c055a5c2feb4055d6d5c7.jpg)

# E.2 PROOF FOR THE DIAGONAL CASE

Now we only need to prove Theorem E.1. Let  $e_1, \ldots, e_d$  be the standard basis. Then  $\tilde{u}_1 = \tilde{v}_1 = e_1$  in this diagonal case. We only use  $e_1$  to stand for  $\tilde{u}_1$  and  $\tilde{v}_1$  in the rest of our analysis.

Let  $R > 0$ . Since  $\pmb{g}(\pmb{\theta})$  is  $\mathcal{C}^2$ -smooth, there exists  $\beta > 0$  such that

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) - \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {\theta} + \boldsymbol {h}) \right\| _ {2} \leq \beta \| \boldsymbol {h} \| _ {2} \tag {21}
$$

for all  $\| \pmb {\theta}\| _2,\| \pmb {\theta} + \pmb {h}\| _2\leq R$  . Then the following can be proved by integration:

$$
\left. \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} + \boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) = \left(\int_ {0} ^ {1} \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {\theta} + \xi \boldsymbol {h}) \mathrm {d} \xi\right) \boldsymbol {h}, \right. \tag {22}
$$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} + \boldsymbol {h}) - \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) - \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \boldsymbol {h} \right\| _ {2} \leq \beta \| \boldsymbol {h} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \tag {23}
$$

By (23), we also have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) - \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {0}) \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {2} = \left\| \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) - \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {0}) - \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {0}) \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {2} \leq \beta \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \tag {24}
$$

Let  $\kappa \coloneqq \beta /\tilde{\mu}_1$ . We assume WLOG that  $R\leq 1 / \kappa$ . Let  $F(x) = \log x - \log (1 + \kappa x)$ . It is easy to see that  $F^{\prime}(x) = \frac{1}{x + \kappa x^{2}}$  and  $F(x)$  is an increasing function with range  $(-\infty ,\log (1 / \kappa))$ . We use  $F^{-1}(y)$  to denote the inverse function of  $F(x)$ . Define  $T_{\alpha}(r)\coloneqq \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\left(F(r) - F(\alpha)\right) = \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\left(\log \frac{r}{\alpha} -\log \frac{1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa\alpha}\right)$ .

Our proof only relies on the following properties of  $J(\mathbf{0})$  (besides that  $\tilde{\mu}_1, e_1$  are the top eigenvalue and eigenvector of  $J(\mathbf{0})$ ):

Lemma E.2. For  $J(\mathbf{0})\coloneqq \mathrm{diag}(\tilde{\mu}_1,\dots ,\tilde{\mu}_d)$  , we have

1. For any  $\pmb{h} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ ,  $\pmb{h}^\top \pmb{J}(\pmb{0})\pmb{h} \leq \tilde{\mu}_1\| \pmb{h}\|_2^2$ ;  
2. For any  $t \geq 0$ ,  $\left\|e^{t\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{0})}-e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1} t} \mathbf{e}_{1} \mathbf{e}_{1}^{\top}\right\|_{2}=e^{\tilde{\mu}_{2} t}$ .

Proof. For Item 1,  $\pmb{h}^{\top}\pmb{J}(\pmb{0})\pmb{h} = \sum_{i=1}^{d}\tilde{\mu}_{i}h_{i}^{2} \leq \tilde{\mu}_{1}\|\pmb{h}\|_{2}^{2}$ . For Item 2,  $\left\|e^{t\pmb{J}(\pmb{0})}-e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}\pmb{e}_{1}\pmb{e}_{1}^{\top}\right\|_{2}= \left\|\mathrm{diag}(0,e^{\tilde{\mu}_{2}t},\ldots,e^{\tilde{\mu}_{d}t})\right\|_{2}=e^{\tilde{\mu}_{2}t}$ .

Lemma E.3. For  $\pmb{\theta}(t) = \phi(\pmb{\theta}_0, t)$  with  $\|\pmb{\theta}_0\|_2 \leq \alpha$  and  $t \leq T_\alpha(r)$ ,

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \right\| _ {2} \leq \frac {1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha \cdot e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} t} \leq r.
$$

Proof. By (24) and Lemma E.2, we have

$$
\frac {1}{2} \frac {\mathrm {d} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\mathrm {d} t} = \langle \boldsymbol {\theta} (t), \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)) \rangle \leq \langle \boldsymbol {\theta} (t), \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \rangle + \beta \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {3} \leq \tilde {\mu} _ {1} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \beta \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {3}.
$$

This implies  $\frac{\mathrm{d}\|\pmb{\theta}(t)\|_2}{\mathrm{d}t} \leq \tilde{\mu}_1(\|\pmb{\theta}(t)\|_2 + \kappa \|\pmb{\theta}(t)\|_2^2)$ . Since  $F'(x) = \frac{1}{x + \kappa x^2}$ , we further have

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} t} F \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \| _ {2}\right) \leq \tilde {\mu} _ {1}.
$$

So  $F(\| \pmb{\theta}(t) \|_2) \leq F(\alpha) + \tilde{\mu}_1 t$ . By definition of  $T_{\alpha}(r)$ , we then know that  $\| \pmb{\theta}(t) \|_2 \leq r$  for all  $t \leq T_{\alpha}(r)$ . So

$$
\log \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \| _ {2} \leq F (\| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \| _ {2}) + \log (1 + \kappa r) \leq F (\alpha) + \tilde {\mu} _ {1} t + \log (1 + \kappa r).
$$

Expending  $F(\alpha)$  proves the lemma.

Lemma E.4. For  $\pmb{\theta}(t) = \phi(\pmb{\theta}_0, t)$  with  $\|\pmb{\theta}_0\|_2 \leq \alpha$  and  $t \leq T_{\alpha}(r)$ , we have

$$
\boldsymbol {\theta} (t) = e ^ {t \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0})} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} + O (r ^ {2}).
$$

Proof. Let  $\hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t) = e^{t\pmb{J}(0)}\pmb{\theta}_0$ . Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{2} \frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} t} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \left\langle \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)) - \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t), \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \right\rangle \\ = \left\langle \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)) - \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) \boldsymbol {\theta} (t), \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \right\rangle + \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t)\right) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t)) \\ \leq \| \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)) - \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \| _ {2} \cdot \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \| _ {2} + \tilde {\mu} _ {1} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is due to Lemma E.2. By (24) and Lemma E.3, we have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)) - \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \right\| _ {2} \leq \beta \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \beta \left(\frac {1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha\right) ^ {2} \cdot e ^ {2 \tilde {\mu} _ {1} t}.
$$

So we have  $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\pmb{\theta}(t) - \hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t)\|_2 \leq \beta \left(\frac{1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa\alpha}\alpha\right)^2 \cdot e^{2\tilde{\mu}_1t} + \tilde{\mu}_1\|\pmb{\theta}(t) - \hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t)\|_2$ . By Gronwall's inequality,

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \right\| _ {2} \leq \int_ {0} ^ {t} \beta \left(\frac {1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha\right) ^ {2} \cdot e ^ {2 \tilde {\mu} _ {1} \tau} e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} (t - \tau)} d \tau .
$$

Evaluating the integral gives

$$
\| \pmb {\theta} (t) - \hat {\pmb {\theta}} (t) \| _ {2} \leq \beta \left(\frac {1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha\right) ^ {2} e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} t} \cdot \frac {e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} t} - 1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \leq \kappa \left(\frac {1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha \cdot e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} t}\right) ^ {2} \leq \kappa r ^ {2},
$$

which proves the lemma.

![](images/25c40fcdff55f9dc80d0480c1a8608e7b7af27627c63054254b3f126ea3d7a0d.jpg)

Lemma E.5. Let  $\pmb{\theta}(t) = \phi(\pmb{\theta}_0, t), \hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t) = \phi(\hat{\pmb{\theta}}_0, t)$ . If  $\max\{\|\pmb{\theta}_0\|_2, \|\hat{\pmb{\theta}}_0\|_2\} \leq \alpha$ , then for  $t \leq T_{\alpha}(r)$

$$
\| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \| _ {2} \leq e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} t + \kappa r} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {0} \| _ {2}.
$$

Proof. For  $t \leq T_{\alpha}(r)$ , by (22),

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{2} \frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} t} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left\langle \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)) - \boldsymbol {g} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t)), \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \right\rangle \\ = (\pmb {\theta} (t) - \hat {\pmb {\theta}} (t)) ^ {\top} \left(\int_ {0} ^ {1} \pmb {J} (\pmb {\theta} _ {\xi} (t)) \mathrm {d} \xi\right) (\pmb {\theta} (t) - \hat {\pmb {\theta}} (t)), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\pmb{\theta}_{\xi}(t) \coloneqq \xi \pmb{\theta}(t) + (1 - \xi) \hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t)$ . By Lemma E.3,  $\max \{\|\pmb{\theta}(t)\|_2, \|\hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t)\|_2\} \leq \frac{1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha \cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_1 t}$  for all  $t \leq T_{\alpha}(r)$ . So  $\|\pmb{\theta}_{\xi}(t)\|_2 \leq \frac{1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha \cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_1 t}$ . Combining these with (21) and Lemma E.2, we have

$$
\boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\xi} (t)) \boldsymbol {h} = \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {0}) \boldsymbol {h} + \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\xi} (t)) - \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {0})) \boldsymbol {h} \leq \left(\tilde {\mu} _ {1} + \beta \cdot \frac {1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha \cdot e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} t}\right) \| \boldsymbol {h} \| _ {2} ^ {2},
$$

for all  $\pmb{h} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ . Thus,  $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\pmb{\theta}(t) - \hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t)\|_2 \leq \left(\tilde{\mu}_1 + \beta \cdot \frac{1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa\alpha}\alpha \cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_1t}\right)\|\pmb{\theta}(t) - \hat{\pmb{\theta}}(t)\|_2$ . This implies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \log \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} (t) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (t) \right\| _ {2}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} (0) - \hat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} (0) \right\| _ {2}} \leq \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(\tilde {\mu} _ {1} + \beta \cdot \frac {1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha \cdot e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} \tau}\right) d \tau \\ \leq \tilde {\mu} _ {1} t + \kappa \cdot \frac {1 + \kappa r}{1 + \kappa \alpha} \alpha e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} t} \\ \leq \tilde {\mu} _ {1} t + \kappa r. \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore,  $\| \pmb {\theta}(t) - \hat{\pmb{\theta}} (t)\| _2\leq e^{\tilde{\mu}_1t + \kappa r}\| \pmb {\theta}(0) - \hat{\pmb{\theta}} (0)\| _2$

Lemma E.6. For every  $t \in (-\infty, +\infty)$ ,  $z(t)$  exists and  $z_{\alpha}(t)$  converges to  $z(t)$  in the following rate:

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha} (t) - \boldsymbol {z} (t) \right\| _ {2} = O (\alpha),
$$

where  $O$  hides constants depending on  $g(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $t$ .

Proof. We prove the lemma in the cases of  $t \in (-\infty, F(R) / \tilde{\mu}_1]$  and  $t > F(R) / \tilde{\mu}_1$  respectively.

Case 1. Fix  $t \in (-\infty, F(R) / \tilde{\mu}_1]$ . Let  $\tilde{\alpha}$  be the unique number such that  $\frac{\tilde{\alpha}}{1 + \kappa \tilde{\alpha}} = \alpha$  (i.e.,  $F(\tilde{\alpha}) = \log \alpha$ ). Let  $\alpha'$  be an arbitrary number less than  $\alpha$ . Let  $t_0 := \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_1} \log \frac{\alpha}{\alpha'}$ . Then  $t_0 = \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_1} (F(\tilde{\alpha}) - \log \alpha') \leq T_{\alpha'}(\tilde{\alpha})$ . By Lemma E.4, we have

$$
\left\| \phi \left(\alpha^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1}, t _ {0}\right) - \alpha \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \right\| _ {2} = \left\| \phi \left(\alpha^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1}, t _ {0}\right) - e ^ {t _ {0} \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {0})} \alpha^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \right\| _ {2} = O (\tilde {\alpha} ^ {2}).
$$

Let  $r \coloneqq F^{-1}(\tilde{\mu}_1 t) \leq R$ . Then  $t + \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_1} \log \frac{1}{\alpha} = T_{\tilde{\alpha}}(r)$  if  $\tilde{\alpha} < r$ .

By Lemma E.3,  $\| \phi (\alpha^{\prime}e_{1},t_{0})\|_{2}\leq \tilde{\alpha}$ . Also,  $\| \alpha e_1\| _2 = \frac{\tilde{\alpha}}{1 + \kappa\tilde{\alpha}}\leq \tilde{\alpha}$ . By Lemma E.5,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha} (t) - \boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha^ {\prime}} (t) \right\| _ {2} = \left\| \phi \left(\alpha^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha^ {\prime}}\right) - \phi \left(\alpha \boldsymbol {e} _ {1}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ = \left\| \phi \left(\phi \left(\alpha^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1}, t _ {0}\right), t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) - \phi \left(\alpha \boldsymbol {e} _ {1}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq O \left(\tilde {\alpha} ^ {2} \cdot e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} \left(t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) + \kappa r}\right) \\ \leq O \left(\frac {\tilde {\alpha} ^ {2}}{\alpha}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

For  $\alpha$  small enough, we have  $\tilde{\alpha} = O(\alpha)$ , so for any  $\alpha' \in (0, \alpha)$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha} (t) - \boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha^ {\prime}} (t) \right\| _ {2} = O (\alpha).
$$

This implies that  $\{z_{\alpha}(t)\}$  satisfies Cauchy's criterion for every  $t$ , and thus the limit  $z(t)$  exists for  $t \leq F(R) / \tilde{\mu}_1$ . The convergence rate can be deduced by taking limits for  $\alpha' \to 0$  on both sides.

Case 2. For  $t = F(R) / \tilde{\mu}_1 + \tau$  with  $\tau >0$ ,  $\phi (\pmb {\theta},\tau)$  is locally Lipschitz with respect to  $\pmb{\theta}$ . So

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha} (t) - \boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha^ {\prime}} (t) \right\| _ {2} = \left\| \phi \left(\boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha} (F (R) / \tilde {\mu} _ {1}), \tau\right) - \phi \left(\boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha^ {\prime}} (F (R) / \tilde {\mu} _ {1}), \tau\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ = O \left(\left\| z _ {\alpha} (F (R) / \tilde {\mu} _ {1}) - z _ {\alpha^ {\prime}} (F (R) / \tilde {\mu} _ {1}) \right\| _ {2}\right) \\ = O (\alpha), \\ \end{array}
$$

which proves the lemma for  $t > F(R) / \tilde{\mu}_1$ .

![](images/df5fd6ebcef4802424acb7c0842cb5239af2807fb5be1fa5f0cb0691d57cb5e3.jpg)

Proof for Theorem E.1. The existence of  $\pmb{z}(t) \coloneqq \lim_{\alpha \to 0} \pmb{z}_{\alpha}(t) = \lim_{\alpha \to 0} \phi \left( \alpha \pmb{e}_1, t + \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_1} \log \frac{1}{\alpha} \right)$  has already been proved in Lemma E.6, where we show  $\| \pmb{z}_{\alpha}(t) - \pmb{z}(t) \|_2 = O(\alpha)$ .

By the continuity of  $\phi (\cdot ,t)$  for every  $t\in \mathbb{R}$ , we have

$$
\boldsymbol {z} (t) = \lim  _ {\alpha \rightarrow 0} \phi \left(\alpha \tilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {1}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) = \phi \left(\lim  _ {\alpha \rightarrow 0} \phi \left(\alpha \tilde {\boldsymbol {v}} _ {1}, \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha}\right), t\right) = \phi (\boldsymbol {z} (0), t).
$$

Now it is only left to prove (7). WLOG we can assume that  $\| \pmb{\delta}_{\alpha}\|_{2}$  is decreasing and  $\frac{\alpha}{2} \leq \| \pmb{\delta}_{\alpha}\|_{2} \leq \alpha$  (otherwise we can do reparameterization). Then our goal becomes proving

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\alpha} (t) - \boldsymbol {z} (t) \right\| _ {2} = O \left(\alpha^ {\frac {\tilde {\gamma}}{\overline {{\mu_ {1}}} + \tilde {\gamma}}}\right). \tag {25}
$$

where  $\theta_{\alpha}(t) \coloneqq \phi\left(\delta_{\alpha}, t + \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_1} \log \frac{1}{\langle \delta_{\alpha}, e_1 \rangle}\right)$ . We prove (25) in the cases of  $t \in (-\infty, F(R) / \tilde{\mu}_1]$  and  $t > F(R) / \tilde{\mu}_1$  respectively.

Case 1. Fix  $t \in (-\infty, (F(R) + \log q) / \tilde{\mu}_1]$ . Let  $\tilde{\alpha}_1 = \alpha^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_1 + \tilde{\gamma}}}$ . Let  $\alpha_1 := e^{F(\tilde{\alpha}_1)} = \frac{\tilde{\alpha}_1}{1 + \kappa \tilde{\alpha}_1}$ . Let  $t_0 := \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_1} (F(\tilde{\alpha}_1) - \log \alpha) \leq T_{\|\delta_\alpha\|_2}(\tilde{\alpha}_1)$ . At time  $t_0$ , by Lemma E.2 we have

$$
\left\| e ^ {t _ {0} \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0})} - e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} t _ {0}} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {2} = e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {2} t _ {0}} = e ^ {\frac {\tilde {\mu} _ {2}}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} (F (\tilde {\alpha} _ {1}) - \log \alpha)} = \left(\frac {\alpha_ {1}}{\alpha}\right) ^ {\frac {\tilde {\mu} _ {2}}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}}}. \tag {26}
$$

Let  $q_{\alpha} \coloneqq \left\langle \frac{\delta_{\alpha}}{\alpha}, e_1 \right\rangle$ . By Definition 5.2, there exists  $q > 0$  such that  $q_{\alpha} \geq q$  for all sufficiently small  $\alpha$ . Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \phi \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha}, t _ {0}\right) - \alpha_ {1} q _ {\alpha} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \right\| _ {2} = \left\| \phi \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha}, t _ {0}\right) - e ^ {t _ {0} \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0})} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \left(e ^ {t _ {0} \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0})} - e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} t _ {0}} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top}\right) \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} \right\| _ {2} \\ = O (\tilde {\alpha} _ {1} ^ {2}) + \left(\frac {\alpha_ {1}}{\alpha}\right) ^ {\frac {\tilde {\mu} _ {2}}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}}} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} \| _ {2} \\ = O \left(\tilde {\alpha} _ {1} ^ {2} + \alpha_ {1} ^ {\bar {\mu} _ {2} / \bar {\mu} _ {1}} \alpha^ {1 - \bar {\mu} _ {2} / \bar {\mu} _ {1}}\right) \\ = O \left(\alpha_ {1} ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

Let  $r \coloneqq F^{-1}(\tilde{\mu}_1t + \log \frac{1}{q_\alpha}) \leq R$ . Then  $t + \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_1}\log \frac{1}{\alpha_1q_\alpha} = T_{\tilde{\alpha}}(r)$  if  $\tilde{\alpha} < r$ . By Lemma E.3,  $\| \phi (\pmb {\delta}_{\alpha},t_0)\| _2\leq \tilde{\alpha}_1$ . Also,  $\| \alpha_{1}q_{\alpha}\pmb {e}_{1}\|_{2}\leq \alpha_{1} = \frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}{1 + \kappa\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}\leq \tilde{\alpha}_{1}$ . By Lemma E.5,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \pmb {\theta} _ {\alpha} (t) - \pmb {z} _ {\alpha_ {1}} (t) \right\| _ {2} \leq \left\| \phi \left(\phi (\pmb {\delta} _ {\alpha}, t _ {0}), t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha_ {1} q _ {\alpha}}\right) - \phi \left(\alpha_ {1} q _ {\alpha} \pmb {e} _ {1}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha_ {1} q _ {\alpha}}\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ = O \left(\alpha_ {1} ^ {2} \cdot e ^ {\tilde {\mu} _ {1} \left(t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\alpha_ {1} q _ {\alpha}}\right) + \kappa r}\right) \\ = O \left(\alpha_ {1}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

Combining this with the convergence rate for  $z_{\alpha_1}(t)$ , we have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\alpha} (t) - \boldsymbol {z} (t) \right\| _ {2} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\alpha} (t) - \boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha_ {1}} (t) \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {z} _ {\alpha_ {1}} (t) - \boldsymbol {z} (t) \right\| _ {2} = O (\alpha_ {1}).
$$

Case 2. For  $t = (F(R) + \log q) / \tilde{\mu}_1 + \tau$  with  $\tau >0$ ,  $\phi (\pmb {\theta},\tau)$  is locally Lipschitz with respect to  $\pmb{\theta}$ . So

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\alpha} (t) - \boldsymbol {z} (t) \right\| _ {2} = \left\| \phi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\alpha} \left(\left(F (R) + \log q\right) / \tilde {\mu} _ {1}\right), \tau\right) - \phi \left(\boldsymbol {z} \left(\left(F (R) + \log q\right) / \tilde {\mu} _ {1}\right), \tau\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ = O \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\alpha} \left(\left(F (R) + \log q\right) / \tilde {\mu} _ {1}\right) - \boldsymbol {z} \left(\left(F (R) + \log q\right) / \tilde {\mu} _ {1}\right) \right\| _ {2}\right) \\ = O \left(\alpha_ {1}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

which proves (25) for  $t > (F(R) + \log q) / \tilde{\mu}_1$ .

# E.3 EXTENSION TO NON-DIAGONALIZABLE CASE

The proof in Appendix E.2 can be generalized to the case where  $J(\mathbf{0})$ . Now we state the theorem formally and sketch the proof idea. We use the notations  $g(\pmb{\theta}), \phi(\pmb{\theta}_0, t), J(\pmb{\theta})$  as in Section 5.1, but we do not assume that  $J(\mathbf{0})$  is diagonalizable. Instead, we use  $\tilde{\mu}_1, \tilde{\mu}_2, \ldots, \tilde{\mu}_d \in \mathbb{C}$  to denote the eigenvalues of  $J(\mathbf{0})$ , repeated according to algebraic multiplicity. We sort the eigenvalues in the descending order of the real part of each eigenvalue, i.e.,  $\Re(\tilde{\mu}_1) \geq \Re(\tilde{\mu}_2) \geq \dots \geq \Re(\tilde{\mu}_d)$ , where  $\Re(z)$  stands for the real part of a complex number  $z \in \mathbb{C}$ . We call the eigenvalue with the largest real part the top eigenvalue.

Theorem E.7. Assume that  $\theta = 0$  is a critical point and the following regularity conditions hold:

1.  $\pmb{g}(\pmb{\theta})$  is  $\mathcal{C}^2$ -smooth;  
2.  $\phi (\pmb {\theta}_0,t)$  exists for all  $\pmb{\theta}_{0}$  and  $t$  
3. The top eigenvalue of  $J(\mathbf{0})$  is unique and is a positive real number, i.e.,

$$
\tilde {\mu} _ {1} > \max  \left\{\Re \left(\tilde {\mu} _ {2}\right), 0 \right\}.
$$

Let  $\tilde{\pmb{v}}_1, \tilde{\pmb{u}}_1$  be the left and right eigenvectors associated with  $\tilde{\mu}_1$ , satisfying  $\tilde{\pmb{u}}_1^\top \tilde{\pmb{v}}_1 = 1$ . Let  $z_{\alpha}(t) \coloneqq \phi(\alpha \tilde{\pmb{v}}_1, t + \frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_1} \log \frac{1}{\alpha})$  for every  $\alpha > 0$ , then  $\forall t \in \mathbb{R}$ ,  $z(t) \coloneqq \lim_{\alpha \to 0} z_{\alpha}(t)$  exists and  $z(t) = \phi(z(0), t)$ . If  $\delta_{\alpha}$  converges to  $\mathbf{0}$  with positive alignment with  $\tilde{\pmb{u}}_1$  as  $\alpha \to 0$ , then for any  $t \in \mathbb{R}$  and for any  $\epsilon > 0$ , there is a constant  $C > 0$  such that for every sufficiently small  $\alpha$ ,

$$
\left\| \phi \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\langle \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} , \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} \rangle}\right) - \boldsymbol {z} (t) \right\| _ {2} \leq C \cdot \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {\tilde {\gamma}}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1} + \tilde {\gamma}} - \epsilon}, \tag {27}
$$

where  $\tilde{\gamma} \coloneqq \tilde{\mu}_1 - \Re(\tilde{\mu}_2)$  is the eigenvalue gap.

Proof Sketch. Define the following two types of matrices. For  $r \geq 1, a, \delta \in \mathbb{R}$ , we define

$$
\boldsymbol {J} _ {a, \delta} ^ {(r)} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} a & \delta & & & & \\ & a & \delta & & \\ & & a & \delta & & \\ & & & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & & & & a & \delta \\ & & & & & a \end{array} \right] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {r \times r}.
$$

For  $r\geq 1,a,b,\delta \in \mathbb{R}$  , we define

$$
\boldsymbol {J} _ {a, b, \delta} ^ {(r)} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} \boldsymbol {C} & \delta \boldsymbol {I} & & & & \\ & \boldsymbol {C} & \delta \boldsymbol {I} & & & \\ & & \boldsymbol {C} & \delta \boldsymbol {I} & & \\ & & & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & & & & \boldsymbol {C} & \delta \boldsymbol {I} \\ & & & & & \boldsymbol {C} \end{array} \right] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {2 r \times 2 r},
$$

where  $C = \left[ \begin{array}{cc}a & -b\\ b & a \end{array} \right]\in \mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}$

By linear algebra, the real matrix  $J(\mathbf{0})$  can be written in the real Jordan normal form, i.e.,  $J(\mathbf{0}) = \tilde{\mathbf{V}}\mathrm{diag}(J_{[1]},\ldots ,J_{[m]})\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{-1}$ , where  $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\in \mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$  is an invertible matrix, and each  $J_{[j]}$  is a real Jordan block. Recall that there are two types of real Jordan blocks,  $J_{a,1}^{(r)}$  or  $J_{a,b,1}^{(r)}$ . The former one is associated with a real eigenvalue  $a$ , and the latter one is associated with a pair of complex eigenvalues  $a\pm bi$ . The sum of sizes of all Jordan blocks corresponding to a real eigenvalue  $a$  is its algebraic multiplicity. The sum of sizes of all Jordan blocks corresponding to a pair of complex eigenvalues  $a\pm bi$  is two times the algebraic multiplicity of  $a + bi$  or  $a - bi$  (note that  $a\pm bi$  have the same multiplicity).

It is easy to see that  $J_{a,\delta}^{(r)} = D J_{a,1}^{(r)}D^{-1}$  for  $D = \mathrm{diag}(\delta^r,\delta^{r - 1},\ldots ,\delta)\in \mathbb{R}^{r\times r}$  and  $J_{a,b,\delta}^{(r)} = D J_{a,b,1}^{(r)}D^{-1}$  for  $D = \mathrm{diag}(\delta^r,\delta^r,\delta^{r - 1},\delta^{r - 1},\ldots ,\delta ,\delta)\in \mathbb{R}^{2r\times 2r}$ . This means for every  $\delta >0$  there exists  $\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\delta}$  such that  $J(\mathbf{0}) = \tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{J}_{\delta}\tilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}$ , where  $J_{\delta}\coloneqq \mathrm{diag}(J_{\delta [1]},\dots ,J_{\delta [m]}),J_{\delta [j]}\coloneqq J_{a,\delta}^{(r)}$  if  $J_{[j]}\coloneqq J_{a,1}^{(r)}$ , or  $J_{\delta [j]}\coloneqq J_{a,b,\delta}^{(r)}$  if  $J_{[j]}\coloneqq J_{a,b,1}^{(r)}$ . Since the top eigenvalue of  $J(\mathbf{0})$  is positive and unique,  $\tilde{\mu}_1$  corresponds to only one block  $[\tilde{\mu}_1]\in \mathbb{R}^{1\times 1}$ . WLOG we let  $J_{1} = [\tilde{\mu}_{1}]$ , and thus  $J_{\delta [1]} = [\tilde{\mu}_1]$ .

We only need to select a parameter  $\delta > 0$  and prove the theorem in the case of  $J(\mathbf{0}) = J_{\delta}$  since we can change the basis in a similar way as we have done in Appendix E.1. By scrutinizing the proof for Theorem E.1, we can find that we only need to reprove Lemma E.2. However, Lemma E.2 may not be correct since  $J(\mathbf{0})$  is not diagonal anymore. Instead, we prove the following:

1. If  $\delta \in (0, \tilde{\gamma})$ , then  $\pmb{h}^{\top} \pmb{J}_{\delta} \pmb{h} \leq \tilde{\mu}_1 \| \pmb{h} \|_2^2$  for all  $\pmb{h} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ ;  
2. For any  $\tilde{\mu}_2^{\prime}\in (\Re (\tilde{\mu}_2),\tilde{\mu}_1)$  , if  $\delta \in (0,\tilde{\mu}_2^\prime -\Re (\tilde{\mu}_2))$  , then  $\left\| e^{t\pmb{J}_{\delta}} - e^{\tilde{\mu}_1t}\pmb {e}_1\pmb {e}_1^\top \right\| _2\leq e^{\tilde{\mu}_2^{\prime}t}$  for all  $t\geq 0$

Proof for Item 1. Let  $\mathcal{K}$  be the set of pairs  $(k_{1}, k_{2})$  such that  $k_{1} \neq k_{2}$  and the entry of  $J_{\delta}$  at the  $k_{1}$ -th row and the  $k_{2}$ -th column is non-zero. Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {J} _ {\delta} \boldsymbol {h} = \boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \frac {\boldsymbol {J} _ {\delta} + \boldsymbol {J} _ {\delta} ^ {\top}}{2} \boldsymbol {h} = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {d} \Re (\tilde {\mu} _ {k}) h _ {k} ^ {2} + \sum_ {(k _ {1}, k _ {2}) \in \mathcal {K}} h _ {k _ {1}} h _ {k _ {2}} \delta \\ \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {d} \Re (\tilde {\mu} _ {k}) h _ {k} ^ {2} + \sum_ {(k _ {1}, k _ {2}) \in \mathcal {K}} \frac {h _ {k _ {1}} ^ {2} + h _ {k _ {2}} ^ {2}}{2} \delta . \\ \end{array}
$$

Note that  $\Re (\tilde{\mu}_k)\leq \Re (\tilde{\mu}_2)$  for  $k\geq 2$  . Also note that there is no pair in  $\mathcal{K}$  has  $k_{1} = 1$  or  $k_{2} = 1$  , and for every  $k\geq 2$  there are at most two pairs in  $\mathcal{K}$  has  $k_{1} = k$  or  $k_{2} = k$  . Combining all these together gives

$$
\boldsymbol {h} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {J} _ {\delta} \boldsymbol {h} \leq \tilde {\mu} _ {1} h _ {1} ^ {2} + (\Re (\tilde {\mu} _ {2}) + \delta) \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {d} h _ {k} ^ {2} \leq \tilde {\mu} _ {1} \| \boldsymbol {h} \| _ {2} ^ {2},
$$

which proves Item 1.

Proof for Item 2. Since  $J_{\delta}$  is block diagonal, we only need to prove that  $\| e^{tJ_{\delta [j]}}\|_2 \leq e^{\tilde{\mu}_2't}$  for every  $j \geq 2$ . If  $J_{\delta [j]} = J_{a,\delta}^{(r)} = aI + \delta N$ , where  $N$  is the nilpotent matrix, then

$$
e ^ {t \boldsymbol {J} _ {\delta [ j ]}} = e ^ {a t \boldsymbol {I} + \delta t \boldsymbol {N}} = e ^ {a t \boldsymbol {I}} e ^ {\delta t \boldsymbol {N}} = e ^ {a t} e ^ {\delta t \boldsymbol {N}},
$$

where the second equality uses the fact that  $I$  and  $N$  are commutable. So we have

$$
\left\| e ^ {t \boldsymbol {J} _ {\delta [ j ]}} \right\| _ {2} \leq e ^ {a t} \left\| e ^ {\delta t \boldsymbol {N}} \right\| _ {2} = e ^ {a t} e ^ {\delta t \left\| \boldsymbol {N} \right\| _ {2}} \leq e ^ {(a + \delta) t}.
$$

If  $J_{\delta [j]} = J_{a,\delta}^{(r)} = D + \delta N^2$ , where  $D = \mathrm{diag}(C,C,\ldots ,C)$  and  $N$  is the nilpotent matrix, then

$$
e ^ {t J _ {\delta [ j ]}} = e ^ {t D + \delta t N ^ {2}} = e ^ {t D} e ^ {\delta t N ^ {2}},
$$

where the second equality uses the fact that  $D$  and  $N^2$  are commutable. Note that  $e^{tC} = e^{at}\left[\begin{array}{cc}\cos(bt) & -\sin(bt)\\ \sin(bt) & \cos(bt)\end{array}\right]$ , which implies  $\|e^{tD}\|_2 = \|e^{tC}\|_2 = e^{at}$ . So we have

$$
\left\| e ^ {t J _ {\delta [ j ]}} \right\| _ {2} \leq \left\| e ^ {t D} \right\| _ {2} \cdot \left\| e ^ {\delta t N ^ {2}} \right\| _ {2} = e ^ {a t} e ^ {\delta t \| N ^ {2} \| _ {2}} \leq e ^ {(a + \delta) t}.
$$

Since  $\delta \in (0, \tilde{\mu}_2' - \Re(\tilde{\mu}_2))$ , we know that  $a + \delta < \tilde{\mu}_2'$ , which completes the proof.

Proof for a fixed  $\delta$ . Since Item 1 continues to hold for  $\delta \in (0, \tilde{\gamma})$ , Lemmas E.3 to E.6 also hold. This proves that  $z(t)$  exists and satisfies (6).

It remains to prove (27) for any  $\epsilon > 0$ . Let  $\tilde{\gamma}' \in (0, \tilde{\gamma})$  be a number such that  $\frac{\tilde{\gamma}'}{\tilde{\mu}_1 + \tilde{\gamma}'} \geq \frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_1 + \tilde{\gamma}} - \epsilon$ . Fix  $\tilde{\mu}_2' = \tilde{\mu}_1 - \tilde{\gamma}'$ ,  $\delta = \tilde{\mu}_2' - \Re(\tilde{\mu}_2)$ . By Item 2, we have  $\left\| e^{tJ_{\delta}} - e^{\tilde{\mu}_1 t}e_1e_1^\top\right\|_2 \leq e^{\tilde{\mu}_2 t}$  for all  $t \geq 0$ . By scrutinizing the proof for Theorem E.1, we can find that the only place we use Item 2 in Lemma E.2 is in (26). For proving (27), we can repeat the proof while replacing all the occurrences of  $\tilde{\mu}_2$  by  $\tilde{\mu}_2'$ . Then we know that for every  $t \in \mathbb{R}$ , there is a constant  $C > 0$  such that

$$
\left\| \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} ^ {- 1} \phi \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\langle \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} , \tilde {\boldsymbol {u}} _ {1} \rangle}\right) - \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {z} (t) \right\| _ {2} \leq C \cdot \| \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {\tilde {\gamma} ^ {\prime}}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1} + \tilde {\gamma} ^ {\prime}}},
$$

for every sufficiently small  $\alpha$ . By definition of  $\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}$ ,  $\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mu}_1 + \tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}} \geq \frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_1 + \tilde{\gamma}} - \epsilon$ . Since  $\delta_{\alpha} \to 0$  as  $\alpha \to 0$ , we have  $\| \tilde{V}_{\delta}^{-1}\delta_{\alpha}\|_{2} < 1$  for sufficiently small  $\alpha$ . Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \phi \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\langle \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} , \hat {\boldsymbol {u}} _ {1} \rangle}\right) - \boldsymbol {z} (t) \right\| _ {2} \leq \| \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} \| _ {2} \cdot \left\| \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} ^ {- 1} \phi \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha}, t + \frac {1}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\langle \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} , \hat {\boldsymbol {u}} _ {1} \rangle}\right) - \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {z} (t) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq \| \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} \| _ {2} \cdot C \cdot \| \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {\bar {\gamma} ^ {\prime}}{\mu_ {1} + \bar {\gamma} ^ {\prime}}} \\ \leq C \cdot \| \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} \| _ {2} \cdot \| \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {\delta} ^ {- 1} \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {\tilde {\gamma} ^ {\prime}}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1} + \tilde {\gamma} ^ {\prime}}} \cdot \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {\alpha} \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {\tilde {\gamma}}{\tilde {\mu} _ {1} + \tilde {\gamma}} - \epsilon}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Absorbing  $\| \tilde{V}_{\delta}\|_{2}\cdot \| \tilde{V}_{\delta}^{-1}\|_{2}^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1} + \tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}}$  into  $C$  proves (27).

# F EIGENVALUES OF JACOBIANS AND HESSIANS

In this section we analyze the eigenvalues of the Jacobian  $J(W)$  at critical points of (2).

For notation simplicity, we write  $\mathrm{sz}(A) \coloneqq A + A^\top$  to denote the symmetric matrix produced by adding up  $A$  and its transpose, and write  $\operatorname{ac}\{A, B\} = AB + BA$  to denote the anticommutator of two matrices  $A, B$ . Then  $g(W)$  can be written as  $g(W) \coloneqq -\operatorname{ac}\{\nabla f(W), W\}$ .

Let  $\pmb{U}_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times r}$  be a stationary point of the function  $\mathcal{L}: \mathbb{R}^{d \times r} \to \mathbb{R}, \mathcal{L}(\pmb{U}) = \frac{1}{2} f(\pmb{U}\pmb{U}^\top)$ , i.e.,  $\nabla \mathcal{L}(\pmb{U}_0) = \nabla f(\pmb{U}_0\pmb{U}_0^\top)\pmb{U}_0 = \pmb{0}$ . By Lemma A.1, this implies

$$
\nabla f \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}\right) \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} = \mathbf {0} \tag {28}
$$

for  $\pmb{W}_0 \coloneqq \pmb{U}_0\pmb{U}_0^\top$ , and thus  $\pmb{W}_0$  is a critical point of (2).

For a real-valued or vector-valued function  $F(\pmb{\theta})$ , we use  $DF(\pmb{\theta})[\pmb{\delta}]$ ,  $D^2 F(\pmb{\theta})[\pmb{\delta}_1, \pmb{\delta}_2]$  to denote the first- and second-order directional derivatives of  $F(\cdot)$  at  $\pmb{\theta}$ .

Let  $\mathcal{X}$  be a linear space, which can be  $\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$  or  $\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}$ . For a function  $F:\mathcal{X}\to \mathcal{X}$ , we use  $DF(\pmb {\theta})$  to denote the directional derivative of  $F$  at  $\pmb{\theta}$ , represented by the linear operator

$$
D F (\boldsymbol {\theta}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ]: \mathcal {X} \rightarrow \mathcal {X}, \boldsymbol {\Delta} \mapsto D F (\boldsymbol {\theta}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = \lim  _ {t \rightarrow 0} \frac {F (\boldsymbol {\theta} + t \boldsymbol {\Delta}) - F (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{t}.
$$

We also write  $DF(\pmb {\theta})[\pmb{\Delta}_1,\pmb {\Delta}_2]\coloneqq \langle DF(\pmb {\theta})[\pmb {\Delta}_1],\pmb {\Delta}_2\rangle$

For a function  $F: \mathcal{X} \to \mathbb{R}$ , we use  $D^{2}F(\pmb{\theta}) = D(\nabla F(\pmb{\theta}))$  to denote the second directional derivative of  $F$  at  $\pmb{\theta}$ , i.e.,  $D^{2}F(\pmb{\theta})[\pmb{\Delta}] = D(\nabla F(\pmb{\theta}))[\pmb{\Delta}], D^{2}F(\pmb{\theta})[\pmb{\Delta}_{1},\pmb{\Delta}_{2}] = D(\nabla F(\pmb{\theta}))[\pmb{\Delta}_{1},\pmb{\Delta}_{2}]$ .

Define  $J(\mathbf{W}) \coloneqq D\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{W})$ . By simple calculus, we can compute the formula for  $J(\mathbf{W}_0)$ :

$$
\boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = - \mathrm {a c} \left\{\nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}), \boldsymbol {\Delta} \right\} - \mathrm {a c} \left\{D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ], \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\},
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {2} ] = - \left\langle \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}), \operatorname {s z} (\boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {1} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {2} ^ {\top}) \right\rangle - D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {1}, \operatorname {s z} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {2} ^ {\top}) ],
$$

where  $\pmb{\Delta},\pmb{\Delta}_1,\pmb{\Delta}_2\in \mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$

We can also compute the formula for  $D^2\mathcal{L}(\pmb {U}_0)$

$$
D ^ {2} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {U} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \boldsymbol {\Delta} + D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \mathrm {s z} (\boldsymbol {\Delta} \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} ^ {\top}) ] \boldsymbol {U} _ {0},
$$

$$
D ^ {2} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {U} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {2} ] = \frac {1}{2} \left(\left\langle \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}), \mathrm {s z} (\boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {1} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {2} ^ {\top}) \right\rangle + D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \mathrm {s z} (\boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {1} \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} ^ {\top}), \mathrm {s z} (\boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {2} \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} ^ {\top}) ]\right),
$$

where  $\pmb{\Delta},\pmb{\Delta}_1,\pmb{\Delta}_2\in \mathbb{R}^{d\times r}$

# F.1 EIGENVALUES AT THE ORIGIN

The eigenvalues of  $J(\mathbf{0})$  is given in Lemma 5.4. Now we provide the proof.

Proof for Lemma 5.4. For  $\mathbf{W}_0 = \mathbf{0}$ , we have

$$
\boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = - \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) \boldsymbol {\Delta} - \boldsymbol {\Delta} \nabla f (\mathbf {0})
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) \left[ \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {2} \right] = - \left\langle \nabla f (\mathbf {0}), \operatorname {s z} \left(\boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {1} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {2} ^ {\top}\right) \right\rangle
$$

It is easy to see from the second equation that  $J(\mathbf{0})$  is symmetric.

Let  $-\nabla f(\mathbf{0}) = \sum_{i=1}^{d} \mu_i \mathbf{u}_{1[i]}\mathbf{u}_{1[i]}^\top$  be the eigendecomposition of the symmetric matrix  $-\nabla f(\mathbf{0})$ . Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mu_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\Delta} + \boldsymbol {\Delta} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} ^ {\top}\right) \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {d} \mu_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\Delta} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\Delta} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} ^ {\top}\right) \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {d} \left(\mu_ {i} + \mu_ {j}\right) \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\Delta} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} ^ {\top} \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {d} \left(\mu_ {i} + \mu_ {j}\right) \left\langle \boldsymbol {\Delta}, \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} ^ {\top} \right\rangle \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} ^ {\top}, \\ \end{array}
$$

which proves (8).

For  $\pmb{\Delta} = \pmb{u}_{1[i]}\pmb{u}_{1[j]}^{\top} + \pmb{u}_{1[j]}\pmb{u}_{1[i]}^{\top}$ , we have

$$
\boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = (\mu_ {i} + \mu_ {j}) \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} ^ {\top} + (\mu_ {i} + \mu_ {j}) \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ j ]} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1 [ i ]} ^ {\top} = (\mu_ {i} + \mu_ {j}) \boldsymbol {\Delta}.
$$

So  $\pmb{u}_{1[i]}\pmb{u}_{1[j]}^{\top} + \pmb{u}_{1[j]}\pmb{u}_{1[i]}^{\top}$  is an eigenvector of  $\pmb{J}(\mathbf{0})$  associated with eigenvalue  $\mu_{i} + \mu_{j}$ . Note that  $\{\pmb{u}_{1[i]}\pmb{u}_{1[j]}^{\top} + \pmb{u}_{1[j]}\pmb{u}_{1[i]}^{\top}: i, j \in [d]\}$  spans all the symmetric matrices, so these are all the eigenvectors in the space of symmetric matrices.

For every antisymmetric matrix  $\pmb{\Delta}$  (i.e.,  $\pmb{\Delta} = -\pmb{\Delta}^{\top}$ ), we have

$$
\boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ^ {\top} ] = \boldsymbol {J} (\mathbf {0}) [ - \boldsymbol {\Delta} ].
$$

So  $J(\mathbf{0})[\Delta] = \mathbf{0}$  and every antisymmetric matrix is an eigenvector associated with eigenvalue 0.

Since every matrix can be expressed as the sum of a symmetric matrix and an antisymmetric matrix, we have found all the eigenvalues.

# F.2 EIGENVALUES AT SECOND-ORDER STATIONARY POINTS

Now we study the eigenvalues of  $J(\mathbf{W}_0)$  when  $\mathbf{U}_0$  is a second-order stationary point of  $\mathcal{L}(\cdot)$ , i.e.,  $\nabla \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{U}_0) = \mathbf{0}$ ,  $D^2 \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{U}_0)[\Delta, \Delta] \geq \mathbf{0}$  for all  $\Delta \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times r}$ . We further assume that  $\mathbf{U}_0$  is full-rank, i.e.,  $\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{U}_0) = r$ . This condition is meet if  $\mathbf{W}_0 := \mathbf{U}_0 \mathbf{U}_0^T$  is a local minimizer of  $f(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{S}_d^+$  but not a minimizer in  $\mathbb{S}_d^+$ .

Lemma F.1. For  $r \leq d$ , if  $\mathbf{U}_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times r}$  is a second-order stationary point of  $\mathcal{L}(\cdot)$ , then either  $\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{U}_0) = \mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{W}_0) = r$ , or  $\mathbf{W}_0$  is a minimizer of  $f(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{S}_d^+$ , where  $\mathbf{W}_0 = \mathbf{U}_0 \mathbf{U}_0^\top$ .

Proof. Assume to the contrary that  $U_0$  has rank  $< r$  and  $W_0$  is a minimizer of  $f(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{S}_d^+$ . The former one implies that there exists a unit vector  $\pmb{q} \in \mathbb{R}^r$  such that  $U_0 \pmb{q} = \mathbf{0}$ , and the latter one implies that there exists  $\pmb{v} \in \mathbb{R}^d$  such that  $\pmb{v}^\top \nabla f(\pmb{W}_0) \pmb{v} < 0$  by Lemma A.2.

Let  $\pmb{\Delta} = \pmb{v}\pmb{q}^{\top}$ . Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} D ^ {2} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {U} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta}, \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = \left\langle \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}), \boldsymbol {v} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \right\rangle + \frac {1}{2} D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \left[ \mathrm {s z} (\boldsymbol {v} (\boldsymbol {U} _ {0} \boldsymbol {q}) ^ {\top}), \mathrm {s z} (\boldsymbol {v} (\boldsymbol {U} _ {0} \boldsymbol {q}) ^ {\top}) \right] \\ = \left\langle \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}), \boldsymbol {v v} ^ {\top} \right\rangle + \frac {1}{2} D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \mathbf {0}, \mathbf {0} ] \\ = \left\langle \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}), \boldsymbol {v} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top} \right\rangle . \\ \end{array}
$$

So  $D^2\mathcal{L}(\pmb {U}_0)[\pmb {\Delta},\pmb {\Delta}] < 0$  , which leads to a contradiction.

![](images/c26a57366d723b491fa446e00bad26aecc6b88817bcc9f60b17a267ff9be0d99.jpg)

By (28), the symmetric matrices  $-\nabla f(\mathbf{W}_0)$  and  $\mathbf{W}_0$  commute, so they can be simultaneously diagonalizable. Since (28) also implies that they have different column spans, we can have the following diagonalization:

$$
- \nabla f \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}\right) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d - r} \mu_ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top}, \quad \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} = \sum_ {i = d - r + 1} ^ {d} \mu_ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} \boldsymbol {v} _ {i} ^ {\top}. \tag {29}
$$

First we prove the following lemma on the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the linear operator  $-D^{2}\mathcal{L}(U_{0})$ :

Lemma F.2. For every  $\Delta \in \mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$ , if

$$
\boldsymbol {U} _ {0} \boldsymbol {\Delta} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\Delta} \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} ^ {\top} = \mathbf {0} \tag {30}
$$

then  $\Delta$  is an eigenvector of the linear operator  $-D^{2}\mathcal{L}(\pmb{U}_{0})[\cdot ]:\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\to \mathbb{R}^{d\times r}$  associated with eigenvalue 0. Moreover, the solutions of (30) spans a linear space of dimension  $\frac{r(r - 1)}{2}$ .

Proof. Suppose  $U_0\Delta^\top + \Delta U_0^\top = 0$ . Then we have  $U_0\Delta^\top = -\Delta U_0^\top$ , and thus  $\Delta^\top = -U_0^+\Delta U_0^\top$ , where  $U_0^+$  is the pseudoinverse of the full-rank matrix  $U_0$ . This implies that there is a matrix  $R \in \mathbb{R}^{r \times r}$ , such that  $\Delta = U_0R$ . Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} - D ^ {2} \mathcal {L} (\pmb {U} _ {0}) [ \pmb {\Delta} ] = - \nabla f (\pmb {W} _ {0}) \pmb {U} _ {0} \pmb {R} - D ^ {2} f (\pmb {W} _ {0}) [ \pmb {U} _ {0} \pmb {\Delta} ^ {\top} + \pmb {\Delta} \pmb {U} _ {0} ^ {\top} ] \pmb {U} _ {0} \\ = - \left(\nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \boldsymbol {U} _ {0}\right) \boldsymbol {R} - D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {0} ] \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} \\ = 0. \\ \end{array}
$$

Replacing  $\pmb{\Delta}$  with  $U_0\pmb{R}$  in (30) gives  $\pmb{U}_0(\pmb{R} + \pmb{R}^\top)\pmb{U}_0^\top = \mathbf{0}$ , which is equivalent to  $\pmb{R} = -\pmb{R}^\top$  since  $\pmb{U}_0$  is full-rank. Since the dimension of  $r \times r$  antisymmetric matrices is  $\frac{r(r-1)}{2}$ , the span spanned by the solutions of (30) also has dimension  $\frac{r(r-1)}{2}$ .

Definition F.3 (Eigendecomposition of  $-D^{2}\mathcal{L}(U_{0})$ ). Let

$$
- D ^ {2} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {U} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = \sum_ {p = 1} ^ {r d} \xi_ {p} \left\langle \boldsymbol {E} _ {p}, \boldsymbol {\Delta} \right\rangle \boldsymbol {E} _ {p}
$$

be the eigendecomposition of the symmetric linear operator  $-D^{2}\mathcal{L}(\pmb{U}_{0})[\cdot]:\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\to \mathbb{R}^{d\times r}$ , where  $\xi_{1},\ldots ,\xi_{rd}\in \mathbb{R}$  are eigenvalues,  $\pmb{E}_1,\dots,\pmb{E}_{rd}\in \mathbb{R}^{d\times r}$  are eigenvectors satisfying  $\langle \pmb {E}_p,\pmb {E}_q\rangle = \delta_{pq}$ . We enforce  $\xi_{p}$  to be 0 and  $\pmb{E}_{p}$  to be a solution of (30) for every  $rd - \frac{r(r - 1)}{2} < p\leq rd$

Lemma F.4. Let  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{D \times D}$  be a matrix. If  $\{\hat{\pmb{u}}_1, \dots, \hat{\pmb{u}}_K\}$  is a set of linearly independent left eigenvectors associated with eigenvalues  $\hat{\lambda}_1, \dots, \hat{\lambda}_K$  and  $\{\tilde{\pmb{v}}_1, \dots, \tilde{\pmb{v}}_{D - K}\}$  is a set of linearly independent right eigenvectors associated with eigenvalues  $\tilde{\lambda}_1, \dots, \tilde{\lambda}_{D - K}$ , and  $\langle \hat{\pmb{u}}_i, \tilde{\pmb{v}}_j \rangle = 0$  for all  $1 \leq i \leq K, 1 \leq j \leq D - K$ , then  $\hat{\lambda}_1, \dots, \hat{\lambda}_K, \tilde{\lambda}_1, \dots, \tilde{\lambda}_{D - K}$  are all the eigenvalues of  $\mathbf{A}$ .

Proof. Let  $\hat{\pmb{U}}\coloneqq (\hat{\pmb{u}}_1,\dots ,\hat{\pmb{u}}_K)^\top \in \mathbb{R}^{K\times D}$  and  $\tilde{\pmb{V}}\coloneqq (\tilde{\pmb{v}}_1,\dots ,\tilde{\pmb{v}}_{D - K})\in \mathbb{R}^{D\times (D - K)}$ . Then both  $\hat{\pmb{U}}$  and  $\tilde{\pmb{V}}$  are full-rank. Let  $\hat{\pmb{U}}^{+} = \hat{\pmb{U}}^{\top}(\hat{\pmb{U}}\hat{\pmb{U}}^{\top})^{-1},\hat{\pmb{V}}^{+} = (\hat{\pmb{V}}^{\top}\hat{\pmb{V}})^{-1}\hat{\pmb{V}}^{\top}$  be the pseudoinverses of  $\hat{\pmb{U}}$  and  $\tilde{\pmb{V}}$ .

Now we define

$$
\boldsymbol {P} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} \hat {\boldsymbol {U}} \\ \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} ^ {+} \end{array} \right], \quad \boldsymbol {Q} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \hat {\boldsymbol {U}} ^ {+} & \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} \end{array} \right].
$$

Then we have

$$
P Q = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \hat {U} \hat {U} ^ {+} & \hat {U} \tilde {V} \\ \tilde {V} ^ {+} \hat {U} ^ {+} & \tilde {V} ^ {+} \tilde {V} \end{array} \right].
$$

Note that  $\hat{U}\hat{U}^{+} = I_{K},\hat{U}\tilde{V} = 0,\tilde{V}^{+}\hat{U}^{+} = (\tilde{V}^{\top}\tilde{V})^{-1}(\hat{U}\tilde{V})^{\top}(\hat{U}\hat{U}^{\top})^{-1} = 0,\tilde{V}^{+}\tilde{V} = I_{D - K}.$  So  $PQ = I_D$  , or equivalently  $Q = P^{-1}$  . Then we have

$$
\boldsymbol {P} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {A} \boldsymbol {P} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \operatorname {d i a g} (\hat {\lambda} _ {1}, \ldots , \hat {\lambda} _ {K}) & * \\ \boldsymbol {0} & \operatorname {d i a g} (\tilde {\lambda} _ {1}, \ldots , \tilde {\lambda} _ {D - K}) \end{array} \right],
$$

where  $*$  can be any  $K \times (D - K)$  matrix. Since  $P^{-1}AP$  is upper-triangular, we know that  $P^{-1}AP$  has eigenvalues  $\hat{\lambda}_1, \dots, \hat{\lambda}_K, \tilde{\lambda}_1, \dots, \tilde{\lambda}_{D - K}$ , and so does  $A$ .

Theorem F.5. The eigenvalues of  $J(W_0)$  can be fully classified into the following 3 types:

1.  $\mu_{i} + \mu_{j}$  is an eigenvalue for every  $1\leq i\leq j\leq d - r$  , and  $\hat{U}_{ij}\coloneqq \pmb {v}_i\pmb {v}_j^\top +\pmb {v}_j\pmb {v}_i^\top$  is an associated left eigenvector.  
2.  $\xi_{p}$  is an eigenvalue for every  $1\leq p\leq rd - \frac{r(r - 1)}{2}$ , and  $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_p\coloneqq \mathbf{E}_p\mathbf{U}_0^\top +\mathbf{U}_0\mathbf{E}_p^\top$  is an associated right eigenvector:  
3. 0 is an eigenvalue, and any antisymmetric matrix is an associated right eigenvector, which spans a linear space of dimension  $\frac{d(d - 1)}{2}$ .

Proof of Theorem F.5. We first prove each item respectively, and then prove that these are all the eigenvalues of  $J(W_0)$ .

Proof for Item 1. For  $\hat{U}_{ij} = v_{i}v_{j}^{\top} + v_{j}v_{i}^{\top}$ , it is easy to check:

$$
\operatorname {a c} \{- \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}), \hat {\boldsymbol {U}} _ {i j} \} = \left(\lambda_ {i} + \lambda_ {j}\right) \hat {\boldsymbol {U}} _ {i j}
$$

$$
\hat {U} _ {i j} W _ {0} = 0
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \hat {\boldsymbol {U}} _ {i j} = \boldsymbol {0}
$$

So we have

$$
\boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta}, \hat {\boldsymbol {U}} _ {i j} ] = (\lambda_ {i} + \lambda_ {j}) \left\langle \boldsymbol {\Delta}, \hat {\boldsymbol {U}} _ {i j} \right\rangle - D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta}, \mathbf {0} ] = (\lambda_ {i} + \lambda_ {j}) \left\langle \boldsymbol {\Delta}, \hat {\boldsymbol {U}} _ {i j} \right\rangle ,
$$

which shows that  $\hat{U}_{ij}$  is a left eigenvector associated with eigenvalue  $\lambda_{i} + \lambda_{j}$ .

Proof for Item 2. By definition of eigenvector, we have  $-D^{2}\mathcal{L}(\pmb{U}_{0})[\pmb{E}_{p}] = \xi_{p}\pmb{E}_{p}$ , so

$$
\xi_ {p} \boldsymbol {E} _ {p} = - \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \boldsymbol {E} _ {p} - D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} \boldsymbol {E} _ {p} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {E} _ {p} \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} ^ {\top} ] \boldsymbol {U} _ {0}.
$$

Right-multiplying both sides by  $\pmb{U}_0^\top$ , we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \xi_ {p} \boldsymbol {E} _ {p} \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} ^ {\top} = - \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \boldsymbol {E} _ {p} \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} ^ {\top} - D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {p} ] \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \\ = - \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \left(\boldsymbol {E} _ {p} \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {U} _ {0} \boldsymbol {E} _ {p} ^ {\top}\right) - D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {p} ] \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \\ = - \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {p} - D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {p} ] \boldsymbol {W} _ {0}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second equality uses the fact that  $\nabla f(\boldsymbol{W}_0)\boldsymbol{U}_0 = \mathbf{0}$  since  $\boldsymbol{U}_0$  is a critical point. Taking both sides into  $\mathrm{sz}(\cdot)$  gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \xi_ {p} \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {p} = - \mathrm {s z} (\nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {p}) - \mathrm {s z} (D ^ {2} f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \tilde {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {p} ] \boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \\ = \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \hat {\boldsymbol {V}} _ {p} ], \\ \end{array}
$$

which proves that  $\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_p$  is a right eigenvector associated with eigenvalue  $\xi_p$ .

Proof for Item 3. Since  $\nabla f(W)$  is symmetric,  $g(W)$  is also symmetric. For any  $\Delta = -\Delta^{\top}$ ,

$$
\boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ] = \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {\Delta} ^ {\top} ] = \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ - \boldsymbol {\Delta} ].
$$

So  $J(\mathbf{W}_0)[\Delta] = \mathbf{0}$  and  $\Delta$  is an eigenvector associated with eigenvalue 0.

No other eigenvalues. Let  $\mathbb{S}_d$  be the space of symmetric matrices and  $\mathbb{A}_d$  be the space of anti-symmetric matrices. It is easy to see that  $\mathbb{S}_d$  and  $\mathbb{A}_d$  are orthogonal to each other, and  $\mathbb{S}_d$  and  $\mathbb{A}_d$  are invariant subspaces of  $J(W_0)[\Delta]$ . Let  $h: \mathbb{S}_d \to \mathbb{S}_d$ ,  $\Delta \mapsto J(W_0)[\Delta]$  be the linear operator  $J(W_0)[\Delta]$  restricted on symmetric matrices. We only need to prove that  $h$  is diagonalizable.

It is easy to see that  $\{\tilde{U}_{ij}\}$  are linearly independent to each other and thus spans a subspace of  $\mathbb{S}_d$  with dimension  $\frac{(d - r)(d - r + 1)}{2}$ . We can also prove that  $\{\tilde{V}_p\}$  spans a subspace of  $\mathbb{S}_d$  with dimension  $rd - \frac{r(r - 1)}{2}$  by contradiction. Assume to the contrary that there exists scalars  $\alpha_p$  for  $1 \leq p \leq rd - r(r - 1)/2$ , not all zero, such that  $\sum_{p=1}^{rd-r(r-1)/2} \alpha_p \tilde{V}_p = 0$ . Then  $\sum_{p=1}^{rd-r(r-1)/2} \alpha_p E_p$  is a solution of (30). However, this suggests that  $\sum_{p=1}^{rd-r(r-1)/2} \alpha_p E_p$  lies in the span of  $\{E_p\}_{rd-r(r-1)/2 < p \leq rd}$ , which contradicts to the linear independence of  $\{E_p\}_{1 \leq p \leq rd}$ .

Note that

$$
\frac {(d - r) (d - r + 1)}{2} + \left(r d - \frac {r (r - 1)}{2}\right) = \frac {d (d + 1)}{2} = \dim (\mathbb {S} _ {d}).
$$

Also note that  $\left\langle \hat{U}_{ij},\tilde{V}_p\right\rangle = 2\boldsymbol {v}_i^\top \boldsymbol {E}_p\boldsymbol{U}_0^\top \boldsymbol {v}_j + 2\boldsymbol {v}_j^\top \boldsymbol {E}_p\boldsymbol{U}_0^\top \boldsymbol {v}_i = 0.$  By Lemma F.4, Items 1 and 2 give all the eigenvalues of  $h$  , and thus Items 1, 2, 3 give all the eigenvalues of  $J(W_0)$

# G PROOFS FOR THE DEPTH-2 CASE

# G.1 PROOF FOR THEOREM 5.6

Proof for Theorem 5.6. Since  $\mathbf{W}(t)$  is always symmetric, it suffices to study the dynamics of the lower triangle of  $\mathbf{W}(t)$ . For any symmetric matrix  $\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{S}_d$ , let  $\operatorname{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}(\mathbf{W}) \in \mathbb{R}^{\frac{d(d + 1)}{2}}$  be the vector consisting of the  $\frac{d(d + 1)}{2}$  entries of  $\mathbf{W}$  in the lower triangle, permuted according to some fixed order. Let  $\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{W})$  be the function defined in (2), which always maps symmetric matrices to symmetric matrices. Let  $\tilde{\mathbf{g}}: \mathbb{R}^{\frac{d(d + 1)}{2}} \to \mathbb{R}^{\frac{d(d + 1)}{2}}$  be the function such that  $\tilde{\mathbf{g}}(\operatorname{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}(\mathbf{W})) = \operatorname{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}(\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{W}))$  for any  $\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{S}_d$ . For  $\mathbf{W}(t)$  evolving with (2), we view  $\operatorname{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}(\mathbf{W}(t))$  as a dynamical system.

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} t} \operatorname {v e c} _ {\mathrm {L T}} (\boldsymbol {W} (t)) = \tilde {\boldsymbol {g}} (\operatorname {v e c} _ {\mathrm {L T}} (\boldsymbol {W} (t))).
$$

By Lemma 5.4, the spaces of symmetric matrices  $\mathbb{S}_d$  and antisymmetric matrices  $\mathbb{A}_d$  are invariant subspaces of  $J(\mathbf{0})$ , and  $\left\{(\mu_i + \mu_j, \boldsymbol{u}_{1[i]}\boldsymbol{u}_{1[j]}^\top + \boldsymbol{u}_{1[j]}\boldsymbol{u}_{1[i]}^\top)\right\}_{1 \leq i \leq j \leq d}$  is the set of all the eigenvalues and eigenvectors in the invariant subspace  $\mathbb{S}_d$ . Thus,  $\tilde{\mu}_1 := 2\mu_1$  and  $\tilde{\mu}_2 := \mu_1 + \mu_2$  are the largest and second largest eigenvalues of the Jacobian of  $\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}(\cdot)$  at  $\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}(\boldsymbol{W}) = \boldsymbol{0}$ , and  $\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_1 = \tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}_1 = \boldsymbol{u}_1\boldsymbol{u}_1^\top$  are the corresponding left and right eigenvectors of the top eigenvalue. Then it is easy to translate Theorem 5.3 to Theorem 5.6.

# G.2 PROOF FOR THEOREM 5.8

The proof for Theorem 5.8 relies on the following Lemma on the gradient flow around a local minimizer:

Lemma G.1. If  $\bar{\theta}$  is a local minimizer of  $\mathcal{L}(\pmb {\theta})$  and for all  $\| \pmb {\theta} - \bar{\pmb{\theta}}\| _2\leq r$ $\pmb{\theta}$  satisfies Lojasiewicz inequality:

$$
\left\| \nabla \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \right\| _ {2} \geq c \left(\mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) - \mathcal {L} (\bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}})\right) ^ {\mu}
$$

for some  $\mu \in [1/2, 1)$ , then the gradient flow  $\pmb{\theta}(t) = \phi(\pmb{\theta}_0, t)$  converges to a point  $\pmb{\theta}_{\infty}$  near  $\bar{\pmb{\theta}}$  if  $\pmb{\theta}_0$  is close enough to  $\bar{\pmb{\theta}}$ , and the distance can be bounded by  $\| \pmb{\theta}_{\infty} - \bar{\pmb{\theta}} \|_2 = O(\| \pmb{\theta}_0 - \bar{\pmb{\theta}} \|_2^{2(1 - \mu)})$ .

Proof. For every  $t \geq 0$ , if  $\|\pmb{\theta}(t) - \bar{\pmb{\theta}}\|_2 \leq r$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} t} \left(\mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)) - \mathcal {L} (\bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}})\right) ^ {1 - \mu} = (1 - \mu) \left(\mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)) - \mathcal {L} (\bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}})\right) ^ {- \mu} \cdot \left\langle \nabla \mathcal {L}, \frac {d \boldsymbol {\theta}}{d t} \right\rangle \\ = - (1 - \mu) (\mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)) - \mathcal {L} (\bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}})) ^ {- \mu} \cdot \| \nabla \mathcal {L} \| _ {2} \cdot \left\| \frac {d \boldsymbol {\theta}}{d t} \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq - (1 - \mu) c \left\| \frac {d \boldsymbol {\theta}}{d t} \right\| _ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore,  $\| \pmb {\theta}(t) - \pmb {\theta}_0\| _2\leq \int_0^t\left\| \frac{\mathrm{d}\pmb{\theta}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right\| _2\mathrm{d}t\leq \frac{1}{(1 - \mu)c}\mathcal{L}(\pmb {\theta}_0)^{1 - \mu} = O(\| \pmb {\theta}_0 - \bar{\pmb{\theta}}\| _2^{2(1 - \mu)}).$  If we choose  $\| \pmb {\theta}(t) - \bar{\pmb{\theta}}\| _2$  small enough, then  $\| \pmb {\theta}(t) - \bar{\pmb{\theta}}\| _2\leq \| \pmb {\theta}(t) - \pmb {\theta}_0\| _2 + \| \pmb {\theta}_0 - \bar{\pmb{\theta}}\| _2 = O(\| \pmb {\theta}_0 - \bar{\pmb{\theta}}\| _2^{2(1 - \mu)}) < r,$  and thus  $\begin{array}{r}\int_0^{+\infty}\left\| \frac{d\pmb{\theta}}{dt}\right\| _2dt \end{array}$  is convergent and finite. This implies that  $\pmb{\theta}_{\infty}\coloneqq \lim_{t\to +\infty}\pmb {\theta}(t)$  exists and  $\| \pmb {\theta}_{\infty} - \bar{\pmb{\theta}}\|_{2} = O(\| \pmb {\theta}_{0} - \bar{\pmb{\theta}}\|_{2}^{2(1 - \mu)}).$

Proof for Theorem 5.8. Since  $\pmb{W}_1^{\mathrm{G}}(t) \in \mathbb{S}_{d, \leq 1}^+$  satisfies (2), there exists  $\pmb{u}(t) \in \mathbb{R}^d$  such that  $\pmb{u}(t)\pmb{u}(t)^{\top} = \pmb{W}_1^{\mathrm{G}}(t)$  and  $\pmb{u}(t)$  satisfies (1), i.e.,  $\frac{\mathrm{d}\pmb{u}}{\mathrm{d}t} = -\nabla \mathcal{L}(\pmb{u})$ , where  $\mathcal{L}: \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}, \pmb{u} \mapsto \frac{1}{2} f(\pmb{u}\pmb{u}^\top)$ . If  $\pmb{W}_1^{\mathrm{G}}(t)$  does not diverge to infinity, then so does  $\pmb{u}(t)$ . This implies that there is a limit point  $\bar{\pmb{u}}$  of the set  $\{\pmb{u}(t): t \geq 0\}$ .

Let  $\mathcal{U} \coloneqq \{\pmb{u} : \mathcal{L}(\pmb{u}) \geq \mathcal{L}(\bar{\pmb{u}})\}$ . Since  $\mathcal{L}(\pmb{u}(t))$  is non-increasing, we have  $\pmb{u}(t) \in \mathcal{U}$  for all  $t$ . Note that  $\bar{\pmb{u}}$  is a local minimizer of  $\mathcal{L}(\cdot)$  in  $\mathcal{U}$ . By analyticity of  $f(\cdot)$ , Lojasiewicz inequality holds for  $\mathcal{L}(\cdot)$  around  $\bar{\pmb{u}}$  (Lojasiewicz, 1965). Applying Lemma G.1 for  $\mathcal{L}$  restricted on  $\mathcal{U}$ , we know that if  $\pmb{u}(t_0)$  is sufficiently close to  $\bar{\pmb{u}}$ , the remaining length of the trajectory of  $\pmb{u}(t)$  ( $t \geq t_0$ ) is finite and thus  $\lim_{t \to +\infty} \pmb{u}(t)$  exists. As  $\bar{\pmb{u}}$  is a limit point, this limit can only be  $\bar{\pmb{u}}$ . Therefore,  $\overline{\boldsymbol{W}}_1 \coloneqq \lim_{t \to +\infty} \boldsymbol{W}_1^{\mathrm{G}}(t) = \bar{\boldsymbol{u}} \bar{\boldsymbol{u}}^\top$  exists.

If  $\overline{W}_1$  is a minimizer of  $f(\cdot)$ ,  $\overline{U} = (\bar{u}, 0, \dots, 0) \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$  is also a minimizer of  $\mathcal{L}: \mathbb{R}^{d \times d} \to \mathbb{R}$ ,  $U \mapsto \frac{1}{2} f(UU^\top)$ . By analyticity of  $f(\cdot)$ , Łojasiewicz inequality holds for  $\mathcal{L}(\cdot)$  around  $\overline{U}$ . For every  $\epsilon > 0$ , we can always find a time  $t_\epsilon$  such that  $\| u(t_\epsilon) - \bar{u} \|_2 \leq \epsilon / 2$ . On the other hand, by Theorem 5.6, there exists a number  $\alpha_\epsilon$  such that for every  $\alpha < \alpha_\epsilon$ ,

$$
\left\| \phi \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {\alpha}, T \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {\alpha}\right) + t _ {\epsilon}\right) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} ^ {\mathrm {G}} \left(t _ {\epsilon}\right) \right\| _ {2} \leq \epsilon / 2, \quad \text {w h e r e} \quad T (\boldsymbol {W}) := \frac {1}{2 \mu_ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\left\langle \boldsymbol {W} , \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} \boldsymbol {u} _ {1} ^ {\top} \right\rangle}.
$$

Combining these together we have  $\left\| \phi (\pmb{W}_{\alpha},T(\pmb{W}_{\alpha}) + t_{\epsilon}) - \overline{\pmb{W}}_{1}\right\|_{2}\leq \epsilon$

It is easy to construct a factorization  $\phi(\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}, T(\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}) + t_{\epsilon}) \coloneqq \mathbf{U}_{\alpha, \epsilon} \mathbf{U}_{\alpha, \epsilon}^{\top}$  such that  $\left\| \mathbf{U}_{\alpha, \epsilon} - \overline{\mathbf{U}} \right\|_2 = O(\epsilon)$ , e.g., we can find an arbitrary factorization and then right-multiply an orthogonal matrix so that the row vector with the largest norm aligns with the direction of  $\bar{\mathbf{u}}$ . Applying Lemma G.1, we know that gradient flow starting with  $\mathbf{U}_{\alpha, \epsilon}$  converges to a point that is only  $O(\epsilon^{2(1 - \mu)})$  far from  $\bar{\mathbf{u}}$ . So we have

$$
\left\| \lim _ {t \to + \infty} \phi (\boldsymbol {W} _ {\alpha}, T (\boldsymbol {W} _ {\alpha}) + t) - \overline {{\boldsymbol {W}}} _ {1} \right\| _ {2} = O (\epsilon^ {2 (1 - \mu)}).
$$

Taking  $\epsilon \rightarrow 0$  complete the proof.

# G.3 PROOF FOR THEOREM 5.11

Theorem G.2. Let  $\overline{W}$  be a critical point of (2) satisfying that  $\overline{W}$  is a local minimizer of  $f(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{S}_{d,\leq r}^{+}$  for some  $r\geq 1$  but not a minimizer in  $\mathbb{S}_d^+$ . Let  $-\nabla f(\overline{W}) = \sum_{i = 1}^{d}\mu_{i}\pmb{v}_{i}\pmb{v}_{i}^{\top}$  be the eigendecomposition of  $-\nabla f(\overline{W})$ . If  $\mu_{1} > \mu_{2}$ , the following limit exists and is a solution of (2).

$$
\boldsymbol {W} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (t) := \lim _ {\epsilon \to 0} \phi \left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {W}}} + \epsilon \boldsymbol {v} _ {1} \boldsymbol {v} _ {1} ^ {\top}, \frac {1}{2 \mu_ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\epsilon} + t\right).
$$

For  $\{\pmb{W}_{\alpha}\} \subseteq \mathbb{S}_d^+$ , if there exists time  $T_{\alpha} \in \mathbb{R}$  for every  $\alpha$  so that  $\phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha}, T_{\alpha})$  converges to  $\overline{\pmb{W}}$  with positive alignment with the top principal component  $\pmb{v}_1\pmb{v}_1^\top$  as  $\alpha \to 0$ , then  $\forall t \in \mathbb{R}$ ,

$$
\lim  _ {\alpha \rightarrow 0} \phi \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {\alpha}, T _ {\alpha} + \frac {1}{2 \mu_ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\langle \phi (\boldsymbol {W} _ {\alpha} , T _ {\alpha}) , \boldsymbol {v} _ {1} \boldsymbol {v} _ {1} ^ {\top} \rangle} + t\right) = \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (t).
$$

Moreover, there exists a constant  $C > 0$  such that

$$
\left\| \phi \left(\pmb {W} _ {\alpha}, T _ {\alpha} + \frac {1}{2 \mu_ {1}} \log \frac {1}{\langle \phi (\pmb {W} _ {\alpha} , T _ {\alpha}) , \pmb {v} _ {1} \pmb {v} _ {1} ^ {\top} \rangle} + t\right) - \pmb {W} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq C \| \phi (\pmb {W} _ {\alpha}, T _ {\alpha}) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {\frac {\tilde {\gamma}}{2 \mu_ {1} + \tilde {\gamma}}}
$$

for every sufficiently small  $\alpha$ , where  $\tilde{\gamma} \coloneqq 2\mu_1 - \max \{\mu_1 + \mu_2, 0\}$ .

Proof. Following Appendix G.1, we view  $\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}(\pmb {W}(t))$  as a dynamical system.

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d}}{\mathrm {d} t} \operatorname {v e c} _ {\mathrm {L T}} (\boldsymbol {W} (t)) = \tilde {\boldsymbol {g}} (\operatorname {v e c} _ {\mathrm {L T}} (\boldsymbol {W} (t))).
$$

Let  $\overline{\boldsymbol{W}} = \overline{\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}$  be a factorization of  $\overline{\boldsymbol{W}}$ , where  $\overline{\boldsymbol{U}} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times r}$ . Since  $\overline{\boldsymbol{W}}$  is a local minimizer of  $f(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{S}_{d, \leq r}^{+}, \overline{\boldsymbol{U}}$  is also a local minimizer of  $\mathcal{L}: \mathbb{R}^{d \times r} \to \mathbb{R}, \boldsymbol{U} \mapsto \frac{1}{2} f(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top})$ . Since  $\overline{\boldsymbol{W}}$  is not a minimizer of  $f(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{S}_d^+$ , by Lemma F.1,  $\overline{\boldsymbol{U}}$  is full-rank. By Theorem F.5,  $\boldsymbol{J}(\overline{\boldsymbol{W}})$  has eigenvalues  $\mu_i + \mu_j, \xi_p, 0$ . By a similar argument as in Appendix G.1, the Jacobian of  $\tilde{\pmb{g}}$  at  $\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}(\boldsymbol{W}(t))$  has eigenvalues  $\mu_i + \mu_j, \xi_p$ .

Since  $\overline{U}$  is a local minimizer,  $\xi_p \leq 0$  for all  $p$ . If  $\mu_1 > \mu_2$ , then  $2\mu_1$  is the unique largest eigenvalue, and Theorem F.5 shows that  $\operatorname{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}(\boldsymbol{v}_1\boldsymbol{v}_1^\top)$  is a left eigenvector associated with  $2\mu_1$ . The eigenvalue gap  $\tilde{\gamma} := 2\mu_1 - \max \{\mu_1 + \mu_2, \max \{\xi_p : 1 \leq p \leq rd - \frac{r(r-1)}{2}\}\} \geq 2\mu_1 - \max \{\mu_1 + \mu_2, 0\}$ .

Also note that  $\left\langle \phi (W_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}) - \overline{W},\pmb{v}_{1}\pmb{v}_{1}^{\top}\right\rangle = \left\langle \phi (W_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}),\pmb{v}_{1}\pmb{v}_{1}^{\top}\right\rangle$  because  $\langle \overline{W},\pmb {v}_1\pmb {v}_1^\top \rangle = 0$  by (29). If  $\phi (\pmb {W}_\alpha ,T_\alpha)$  converges to  $\overline{W}$  as  $\alpha \rightarrow 0$  , then it has positive alignment with  $\pmb {v}_1\pmb {v}_1^\top$  iff  $\lim \inf_{\alpha \to 0}\frac{\left\langle\phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}),\pmb{v}_{1}\pmb{v}_{1}^{\top}\right\rangle}{\left\|\phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}) - \overline{\pmb{W}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}} >0.$  Then it is easy to translate Theorem 5.3 to Theorem G.2.

# G.4 GRADIENT FLOW ONLY FINDS MINIMIZERS (PROOF FOR THEOREM 5.10)

The proof for Theorem 5.10 is based on the following two theorems from the literature.

Theorem G.3 (Theorem 3.1 in Du and Lee 2018). Let  $f: \mathbb{R}^{d \times d} \to \mathbb{R}$  be a  $\mathcal{C}^2$  convex function. Then  $\mathcal{L}: \mathbb{R}^{d \times k} \to \mathbb{R}$ ,  $\mathcal{L}(U) = f(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{U}^\top)$ ,  $k \geq d$  satisfies that (1). Every local minimizer of  $\mathcal{L}$  is also a global minimizer; (2). All saddles are strict. Here saddles denote those stationary points whose hessian are not positive semi-definite (thus including local maximizers).

Theorem G.4 (Theorem 2 in Lee et al. 2017). Let  $\pmb{g}$  be a  $\mathcal{C}^1$  mapping from  $\mathcal{X} \rightarrow \mathcal{X}$  and  $\operatorname*{det}(D\pmb{g}(\pmb{x})) \neq 0$  for all  $\pmb{x} \in \mathcal{X}$ . Then the set of initial points that converge to an unstable fixed point has measure zero,  $\mu \left( \{\pmb{x}_0 : \lim_{k \to \infty} \pmb{g}^k(\pmb{x}_0) \in \mathcal{A}_g^*\} \right) = 0$ , where  $\mathcal{A}_g^* = \{\pmb{x} : \pmb{g}(\pmb{x}) = \pmb{x}, \max_i |\lambda_i(D\pmb{g}(\pmb{x}))| > 1\}$ .

Theorem G.5 (GF only finds minimizers, a continuous analog of Theorem G.4). Let  $\pmb{f} : \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}^d$  be a  $\mathcal{C}^1$ -smooth function, and  $\phi : \mathbb{R}^d \times \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}^d$  be the solution of the following differential equation,

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \phi (\boldsymbol {x} , t)}{\mathrm {d} t} = \boldsymbol {f} (\phi (\boldsymbol {x}, t)), \quad \phi (\boldsymbol {x}, 0) = \boldsymbol {x}, \quad \forall \boldsymbol {x} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {d}, t \in \mathbb {R}.
$$

Then the set of initial points that converge to a unstable critical point has measure zero,  $\mu\left(\left\{\pmb{x}_0:\lim_{t\to \infty}\phi (\pmb{x}_0,t)\in \mathcal{U}_{\pmb{f}}^*\right\}\right) = 0$  where  $\mathcal{U}_{\pmb{f}}^{*} = \{\pmb {x}:\pmb {f}(\pmb {x}) = \pmb {0},\lambda_{1}(D\pmb {f}(\pmb {x})) > 0\}$  and  $D\pmb{f}$  is the Jacobian matrix of  $\pmb{f}$ .

Proof of Theorem G.5. By Theorem 1 in Section 2.3, Perko (2013), we know  $\phi(\cdot, \cdot)$  is  $\mathcal{C}^1$ -smooth for both  $x, t$ . We let  $\pmb{g}(x) = \phi(x, 1)$ , then we know  $\pmb{g}^{-1}(x) = \phi(x, -1)$  and both  $\pmb{g}, \pmb{g}^{-1}$  are  $\mathcal{C}^1$ -smooth. Note that  $D\pmb{g}^{-1}(x)$  is the inverse matrix of  $D\pmb{g}(x)$ . So both of the two matrices are invertible. Thus we can apply Theorem G.4 and we know  $\mu\left(\left\{x_0 : \lim_{k \to \infty} \pmb{g}^k(x_0) \in \mathcal{A}_{\pmb{g}}^*\right\}\right) = 0$ .

Note that if  $\lim_{t\to \infty}\phi (x,t)$  exists, then  $\lim_{k\to \infty}g^k (\pmb {x}) = \lim_{t\to \infty}\phi (\pmb {x},t)$ . It remains to show that  $\mathcal{U}_f^*\subseteq \mathcal{A}_g^*$ . For  $\pmb {f}(\pmb {x}_0) = \pmb{0}$ , we have  $\phi (\pmb {x}_0,t) = \pmb {x}_0$  and thus  $\pmb {g}(\pmb {x}_0) = \pmb {x}_0$ . Now it suffices to prove that  $\lambda_1(Dg(x_0)) > 1$ . For every  $t\in [0,1]$ , by Corollary of Theorem 1 in Section 2.3, Perko (2013), we have  $\frac{\partial}{\partial t} D\phi (\pmb {x},t) = D\pmb {f}(\phi (\pmb {x},t))D\phi (\pmb {x},t),\forall \pmb {x},t$ . Thus,

$$
\frac {\partial}{\partial t} D \phi (x _ {0}, t) = D \boldsymbol {f} (\phi (x _ {0}, t)) D \phi (x _ {0}, t) = D \boldsymbol {f} (x _ {0}) D \phi (x _ {0}, t).
$$

Solving this ODE gives  $D\pmb{g}(\pmb{x}_0) = D\phi(\pmb{x}, 1) = e^{D\pmb{f}(x_0)}D\phi(\pmb{x}, 0) = e^{D\pmb{f}(x_0)}$ , where the last equality is due to  $D\phi(\pmb{x}, 0) \equiv \pmb{I}$ ,  $\forall \pmb{x}$ . Combining this with  $\lambda_1(D\pmb{f}(x_0)) > 0$ , we have  $\lambda_1(D\pmb{g}(x_0)) > 1$ .

Thus we have  $\mathcal{U}_{\pmb{f}}^{*} := \{\pmb{x}_0 : \pmb{f}(\pmb{x}_0) = \pmb{0}, \lambda_1(D\pmb{f}(\pmb{x}_0)) > 0\} \subseteq \mathcal{A}_{\pmb{g}}^{*}$ , which implies that  $\{\pmb{x}_0 : \lim_{t \to \infty} \phi(\pmb{x}_0, t) \in \mathcal{U}^*\} \subseteq \{\pmb{x}_0 : \lim_{k \to \infty} \pmb{g}^k(\pmb{x}_0) \in \mathcal{A}_{\pmb{g}}^*\}$ .

Theorem 5.10. Let  $f: \mathbb{R}^{d \times d} \to \mathbb{R}$  be a convex  $\mathcal{C}^2$ -smooth function. (1). All stationary points of  $\mathcal{L}: \mathbb{R}^{d \times d} \to \mathbb{R}$ ,  $\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{U}) = \frac{1}{2} f(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^\top)$  are either strict saddles or global minimizers; (2). For any random initialization,  $GF(1)$  converges to strict saddles of  $\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{U})$  with probability 0.

Proof of Theorem 5.10. For (1), by Theorem G.3, we immediately know all the stationary points of  $\mathcal{L}(\cdot)$  are either global minimizers or strict saddles. (2) is just a direct consequence of Theorem G.5 by setting  $\pmb{f}$  in the above proof to  $-\nabla \mathcal{L}$ .

# H EQUIVALENCE BETWEEN GF AND GLRL
