In this section we elaborate on the theoretical evidence that GF and GLRL are equivalent generically, including the case where GLRL does not end in the first phase. The word "generically" used when we want to assume one of the following regularity conditions:

1. We want to assume that GF converges to a local minimizer (i.e., GF does not get stuck on saddle points);  
2. We want to assume that the top eigenvalue  $\lambda_1(-\nabla f(\pmb{W}))$  is unique for a critical point  $\pmb{W}$  of (2) that is not a minimizer of  $f(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{S}_d^+$ ;  
3. We want to assume that a convergent sequence of PSD matrices  $W_{\alpha} \to \overline{W}$  has positive alignment with  $\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}$  for some fixed vector  $\boldsymbol{v}$  with  $\langle \overline{W}, \boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top} \rangle = 0$ , i.e., for a convergent sequence of PSD matrices  $W_{\alpha} \to \overline{W}$ , it holds for sure that  $\lim_{\alpha \to 0} \left\langle \frac{W_{\alpha} - \overline{W}}{\|W_{\alpha} - \overline{W}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}, \boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top} \right\rangle = \lim_{\alpha \to 0} \inf_{\| W_{\alpha} - \overline{W}\|_{\mathrm{F}}} \langle W_{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top} \rangle \geq 0$ , and we further assume that the inequality is strict generically.

Theorem G.2 uncovers how GF with infinitesimal initialization generically behaves. Let  $\overline{W}_0 \coloneqq \mathbf{0}$ . For every  $r \geq 1$ , if  $\overline{W}_{r-1}$  is a local minimizer in  $\mathbb{S}_{d, \leq r-1}^+$  but not a minimizer in  $\mathbb{S}_d^+$ , then  $\lambda_1(-\nabla f(\overline{W}_{r-1})) > 0$  by Lemma A.2. Generically, the top eigenvalue  $\lambda_1(-\nabla f(\overline{W}_{r-1}))$  should

be unique, i.e.,  $\lambda_1(-\nabla f(\overline{\mathbf{W}}_{r - 1})) > \lambda_2(-\nabla f(\overline{\mathbf{W}}_{r - 1}))$ . This enables us to apply Theorem G.2 and deduce that the limiting trajectory

$$
\boldsymbol {W} _ {r} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (t) := \lim  _ {\epsilon \rightarrow 0} \phi \left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {W}}} _ {r - 1} + \epsilon \boldsymbol {u} _ {r} \boldsymbol {u} _ {r} ^ {\top}, \frac {1}{2 \lambda_ {1} (- \nabla f (\overline {{\boldsymbol {W}}} _ {r - 1}))} \log \frac {1}{\epsilon} + t\right)
$$

exists, where  $\pmb{u}_r$  is the top eigenvector of  $-\nabla f(\overline{\pmb{W}}_{r-1})$ . This  $\pmb{W}_r^{\mathrm{G}}(\cdot)$  is exactly the trajectory of GLRL in phase  $r$  as  $\epsilon \to 0$ .

Note that  $\mathbf{W}_r^{\mathrm{G}}(\cdot)$  corresponds to a trajectory of GF minimizing  $\mathcal{L}(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}$ , which should generically converge to a local minimizer of  $\mathcal{L}(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}$ . This means the limit  $\overline{\mathbf{W}}_r\coloneqq \lim_{t\to +\infty}\mathbf{W}_r^{\mathrm{G}}(t)$  should generically be a local minimizer of  $f(\cdot)$  in  $\mathbb{S}_{d,\leq r}^{+}$ . If  $\overline{\mathbf{W}}_r$  is further a minimizer in  $\mathbb{S}_d^+$ , then  $\lambda_{1}(-\nabla f(\overline{\mathbf{W}}_{r}))\leq 0$  and GLRL exits with  $\overline{\mathbf{W}}_r$ ; otherwise GLRL enters phase  $r + 1$ .

If GF aligns well with GLRL in the beginning of phase  $r$  (defined below), then by Theorem G.2, as  $\alpha \to 0$ , the minimum distance from GF to  $\pmb{W}_r^{\mathrm{G}}(t)$  converges to 0 for every  $t \in \mathbb{R}$ . Therefore, GF can get arbitrarily close to the  $r$ -th critical point  $\overline{\pmb{W}}_r$  of GLRL, i.e., there exists a suitable choice  $T_{\alpha}^{(r)}$  so that  $\lim_{\alpha \to 0} \phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha}, T_{\alpha}^{(r)}) = \overline{\pmb{W}}_r$ . Note that  $\langle \overline{\pmb{W}}_r, \pmb{u}_r \pmb{u}_r^\top \rangle = 0$  by (29) and thus  $\lim_{\alpha \to 0} \left\langle \frac{\phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha}, T_{\alpha}^{(r)}) - \overline{\pmb{W}}_r}{\|\phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha}, T_{\alpha}^{(r)}) - \overline{\pmb{W}}_r\|_{\mathrm{F}}}, \pmb{u}_r \pmb{u}_r^\top \right\rangle = \lim_{\alpha \to 0} \inf_{\| \phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha}, T_{\alpha}^{(r)}) - \overline{\pmb{W}}_r\|_{\mathrm{F}}} \frac{\langle \phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha}, T_{\alpha}^{(r)}), \pmb{u}_r \pmb{u}_r^\top \rangle}{\|\phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha}, T_{\alpha}^{(r)}) - \overline{\pmb{W}}_r\|_{\mathrm{F}}} \geq 0$ . Generically, there should exist a suitable choice of  $T_{\alpha}^{(r)}$  so that  $\phi(\pmb{W}_{\alpha}, T_{\alpha}^{(r)})$  not only converges to  $\overline{\pmb{W}}_r$  but also has positive alignment with  $\pmb{u}_r \pmb{u}_r^\top$ , that is, GF should generically align well with GLRL in the beginning of phase  $r + 1$ .

Definition H.1. We say that GF aligns well with GLRL in the beginning of phase  $r$  if there exists  $T_{\alpha}^{(r)}$  for every  $\alpha > 0$  such that  $\phi(\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}, T_{\alpha}^{(r)})$  converges to  $\overline{\mathbf{W}}_{r-1}$  with positive alignment with  $\mathbf{u}_r \mathbf{u}_r^\top$  as  $\alpha \to 0$ .

If the initialization satisfies that  $\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}$  converges to 0 with positive alignment with  $\mathbf{u}_1 \mathbf{u}_1^\top$  as  $\alpha \to 0$ , then GF aligns well with GLRL in the beginning of phase 1, which can be seen by taking  $T_{\alpha}^{(1)} = 0$ . Now assume that GF aligns well with GLRL in the beginning of phase  $r - 1$ , then the above argument shows that GF should generically align well with GLRL in the beginning of phase  $r$ , if GLRL does not exit in phase  $r - 1$ . In the other case, we can use a similar argument as in Theorem 5.8 to show that GF converges to a solution near the minimizer  $\overline{\mathbf{W}}_r$  of  $f(\cdot)$  as  $t \to \infty$ , and the distance between the solution and  $\overline{\mathbf{W}}_r$  converges to 0 as  $\alpha \to 0$ . By this induction we prove that GF with infinitesimal initialization is equivalent to GLRL generically.

# I PROOFS FOR DEEP MATRIX FACTORIZATION

# I.1 PRELIMINARY LEMMAS

Lemma I.1. If  $W(0) \succeq 0$ , then  $W(t) \succeq 0$  and  $\operatorname{rank}(W(t)) = \operatorname{rank}(W(0))$  for all  $t$ .

Proof. Note that we can always find a set of balanced  $\mathbf{U}_i(t)$ , such that  $\mathbf{U}_1(t) \ldots \mathbf{U}_L(t) = \mathbf{W}(t)$ ,  $d_2 = d_3 = \dots = d_L = \mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{W}(t))$  and write the dynamics of  $\mathbf{W}(t)$  in the space of  $\{\mathbf{U}_i\}_{i=1}^L$ . Thus it is clear that for all  $t'$ ,  $\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{W}(t')) \leq \mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{W}(t))$ . We can apply the same argument for  $t'$  and we know  $\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{W}(t)) \leq \mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{W}(t'))$ . Thus  $\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{W}(t))$  is constant over time, and we denote it by  $k$ . Since eigenvalues are continuous matrix functions, and  $\forall t, \lambda_i(\mathbf{W}(t)), i \in [k] \neq 0$ . Thus they cannot change their signs and it must hold that  $\mathbf{W}(t) \succeq \mathbf{0}$ .

Lemma I.2.  $\forall a,b,P\in \mathbb{R},$  if  $a > b\geq 0,P\geq 1$  , then  $\frac{a^P - b^P}{a - b}\leq Pa^{P - 1}.$

Proof. Let  $f(x) = P(1 - x) - (1 - x^P)$ . Since  $f'(x) = -P + Px^{P-1} < 0$  for all  $x \in [0,1)$ ,  $f(x) \geq f(0) = 0$ . Then substituting  $x$  by  $\frac{b}{a}$  completes the proof.

Recall we use  $DF(N)[M]$  to denote the directional derivative along  $M$  of  $\pmb{F}$  at  $\pmb{N}$ .

Lemma I.3. Let  $F:\mathbb{S}_d^+ \to \mathbb{S}_d^+$ ,  $M \mapsto M^P$ , where  $P \geq 1$  and  $P \in \mathbb{Q}$ . Then  $\forall M, N \succeq 0$ ,

$$
\| D \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {N}) [ \boldsymbol {M} ] \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq P \| \boldsymbol {N} \| _ {2} ^ {P - 1} \| \boldsymbol {M} \| _ {\mathrm {F}},
$$

where  $D\pmb {F}(\pmb {N})[\pmb {M}]\coloneqq \lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\pmb{F}(\pmb{N} + t\pmb{M}) - \pmb{F}(\pmb{N})}{t}$  is the directional derivative of  $\pmb{F}$  along  $\pmb{M}$ .

Proof. Let  $\mathbf{N} = \mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Sigma}\mathbf{U}^{\top}$ , where  $\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top} = \mathbf{I}$  and  $\mathbf{\Sigma} = \mathrm{diag}(\sigma_1,\dots ,\sigma_d)$ . Note that  $\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{U}^{\top}) = \mathbf{U}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{M})\mathbf{U}^{\top}$  for any  $\mathbf{M} \in \mathbb{S}_d^+$ . Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| D \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {N}) [ \boldsymbol {M} ] \| _ {\mathrm {F}} = \lim  _ {t \rightarrow 0} \frac {\| \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {N} + t \boldsymbol {M}) - \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {N}) \| _ {\mathrm {F}}}{t} \\ = \lim  _ {t \rightarrow 0} \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma} + t \boldsymbol {U} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {M} \boldsymbol {U}) - \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}}}{t} \\ = \left\| D F (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) [ \boldsymbol {U} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {M} \boldsymbol {U} ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore, it suffices to prove the lemma for the case where  $N$  is diagonal, i.e.,  $N = \Sigma$ .

Assume  $P = \frac{q}{p}$ , where  $p, q \in \mathbb{N}$  and  $q \geq p > 0$ . Define  $G(N) = N^{\frac{1}{p}}$ . Then  $G(\Sigma)^{p} = \Sigma$ . Taking directional derivative on both sides along direction  $M$ , we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \boldsymbol {G} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) ^ {i - 1} D \boldsymbol {G} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) [ \boldsymbol {M} ] \boldsymbol {G} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) ^ {p - 1} = \boldsymbol {M},
$$

So we have

$$
[ D G (\Sigma) [ M ] ] _ {i j} = \frac {m _ {i j}}{\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {p} \sigma_ {i} ^ {\frac {k - 1}{p}} \sigma_ {j} ^ {\frac {p - k}{p}}}.
$$

Let  $H(G) = G^q$ . With the same argument, we know

$$
[ D H (G (\Sigma)) [ M ] ] _ {i j} = m _ {i j} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {q} \sigma_ {i} ^ {\frac {k - 1}{p}} \sigma_ {j} ^ {\frac {q - k}{p}}.
$$

Note that  $\pmb{H}(\pmb{G}(\pmb{\Sigma})) = \pmb{F}(\pmb{\Sigma})$ . By chain rule, we have

$$
D F (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) [ \boldsymbol {M} ] = D H (\boldsymbol {G} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma})) [ D G (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) [ \boldsymbol {M} ] ].
$$

That is,

$$
[ D \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) [ \boldsymbol {M} ] ] _ {i j} = m _ {i j} \frac {\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {q} \sigma_ {i} ^ {\frac {k - 1}{p}} \sigma_ {j} ^ {\frac {q - k}{p}}}{\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {p} \sigma_ {i} ^ {\frac {k - 1}{p}} \sigma_ {j} ^ {\frac {p - k}{p}}}.
$$

When  $\sigma_{i} = \sigma_{j}$ , clearly  $[DF(\Sigma)[M]]_{ij} = m_{ij} \cdot \frac{q}{p} \cdot \sigma_{i}^{\frac{q - p}{p}} = Pm_{ij}\sigma_{i}^{P - 1}$ . Otherwise, we assume WLOG that  $\sigma_{i} > \sigma_{j}$ , we multiply  $\sigma_{i} - \sigma_{j}$  to both numerator and denominator and we have

$$
| [ D \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) [ \boldsymbol {M} ] ] _ {i j} | = | m _ {i j} | \frac {\sigma_ {i} ^ {P} - \sigma_ {j} ^ {P}}{\sigma_ {i} - \sigma_ {j}} \leq | m _ {i j} | P \sigma_ {i} ^ {P - 1} \leq | m _ {i j} | P \| \boldsymbol {\Sigma} \| _ {2} ^ {P - 1}.
$$

where the first inequality is by Lemma I.2. Thus we conclude the proof.

Lemma I.4. For any  $A, B \succeq 0$  and  $P \in \mathbb{R}, P \geq 1$ ,

$$
\left. \right.\left\| \boldsymbol {A} ^ {P} - \boldsymbol {B} ^ {P} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq P \| \boldsymbol {A} - \boldsymbol {B} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \max  \left\{\| \boldsymbol {A} \| _ {2} ^ {P - 1}, \| \boldsymbol {B} \| _ {2} ^ {P - 1} \right\}.
$$

Proof. Since both sides are continuous in  $P$  and  $\mathbb{Q}$  is dense in  $\mathbb{R}$ , it suffices to prove the lemma for  $P \in \mathbb{Q}$ . Let  $\rho := \max \left\{\| A \|_2, \| B \|_2 \right\}$  and  $F(M) = M^P$ . Define  $N: [0,1] \to \mathbb{S}_d^+, N(t) = (1 - t)A + tB$ , we have

1.  $\| N(t)\| _2\leq \rho$  , since  $\| \cdot \| _2$  is convex.

2.  $\|DF(N(t))[B - A]\|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq P\|N(t)\|_{2}^{P-1}\|B - A\|_{\mathrm{F}}$  by Lemma I.3.

Therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {N} (1)) - \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {N} (0)) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \int_ {0} ^ {1} \left\| \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {N} (t))}{\mathrm {d} t} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \mathrm {d} t \\ = \int_ {t = 0} ^ {1} \| D F (N (t)) [ B - A ] \| _ {F} d t \\ \leq P \| \boldsymbol {A} - \boldsymbol {B} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \rho^ {P - 1}, \\ \end{array}
$$

which completes the proof.

![](images/54e91eaf6107de21e19f2d7c0a531f95723ef8f05ad9010ce2f3d4199492c1b0.jpg)

For a locally Lipschitz function  $f(\cdot)$ , the Clarke subdifferential (Clarke, 1975; 1990; Clarke et al., 2008) of  $f$  at any point  $x$  is the following convex set

$$
\frac {\partial^ {\circ} f (\boldsymbol {x})}{\partial \boldsymbol {x}} := \operatorname {c o} \left\{\lim  _ {k \rightarrow \infty} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {x} _ {k}): \boldsymbol {x} _ {k} \rightarrow \boldsymbol {x}, f \text {i s d i f f e r e n t i a b l e a t} \boldsymbol {x} _ {k} \right\},
$$

where co denotes the convex hull.

Clarke subdifferential generalize the standard notion of gradients in the sense that, when  $f$  is smooth,  $\frac{\partial^{\circ}f(\pmb{x})}{\partial\pmb{x}} = \{\nabla f(\pmb {x})\}$ . Clarke subdifferential satisfies the chain rule:

Theorem I.5 (Theorem 2.3.10, Clarke 1990). Let  $\pmb{F} : \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^d$  be a differentiable function and  $g : \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}$  Lipschitz around  $\pmb{F}(\pmb{x})$ . Then  $f = g \circ \pmb{F}$  is Lipschitz around  $\pmb{x}$  and one has

$$
\frac {\partial^ {\circ} f (\boldsymbol {x})}{\partial \boldsymbol {x}} \subseteq \frac {\partial^ {\circ} g (\boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {x}))}{\partial \boldsymbol {F}} \circ \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {F} (\boldsymbol {x})}{\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {x}}.
$$

Let  $\lambda_{m}:\mathbb{S}_{d}\to \mathbb{R},M\mapsto \lambda_{m}(M)$  be the  $m$  -th largest eigenvalue of a symmetric matrix  $M$  . The following theorem gives the Clarke's subdifferentials of the eigenvalue:

Theorem I.6 (Theorem 5.3, Hiriart-Urruty and Lewis 1999). The Clarke subdifferential of the eigenvalue function  $\lambda_{m}$  is given below, where co denotes the convex hull:

$$
\frac {\partial^ {\circ} \lambda_ {m} (\boldsymbol {M})}{\partial \boldsymbol {M}} = \operatorname {c o} \left\{\boldsymbol {v} \boldsymbol {v} ^ {\top}: \boldsymbol {M} \boldsymbol {v} = \lambda_ {m} (\boldsymbol {M}) \boldsymbol {v}, \| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} = 1 \right\}.
$$

# I.2 PROOF OF LEMMA 6.1

Since  $\mathbf{W}(t) \succeq \mathbf{0}$  by Lemma I.1, (11) can be rewritten as the following:

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {W}}{\mathrm {d} t} = - \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {L - 1} \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\frac {2 i}{L}} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \boldsymbol {W} ^ {2 - \frac {2 i + 2}{L}}. \tag {31}
$$

Proof for Lemma 6.1. Suppose  $\pmb{W}(t)$  is a symmetric solution of (11). By Lemma I.1, we know  $\pmb{W}(t)$  also satisfies (31). Now we let  $\pmb{R}(t)$  be the solution of the following ODE with  $\pmb{R}(0) := (\pmb{W}(0))^{\frac{1}{L}}$ . Note we don't define  $\pmb{R}(t)$  by  $(\pmb{W}(t))^{\frac{1}{L}}$ .

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {R}}{\mathrm {d} t} = - \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {L - 1} (- 1) ^ {i} \boldsymbol {R} ^ {i} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {R} ^ {L}) \boldsymbol {R} ^ {L - 1 - i}. \tag {32}
$$

The calculation below shows that  $\pmb{R}^{L}(t)$  also satisfies (31).

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {R} ^ {L}}{\mathrm {d} t} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {L - 1} \boldsymbol {R} ^ {j} \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {R}}{\mathrm {d} t} \boldsymbol {R} ^ {L - 1 - j} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {L - 1} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {L - 1} (- 1) ^ {i} \boldsymbol {R} ^ {i + j} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {R} ^ {L}) \boldsymbol {R} ^ {2 L - 2 - i - j} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {2 L - 2} \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {i} (- 1) ^ {j}\right) \boldsymbol {R} ^ {i} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {R} ^ {L}) \boldsymbol {R} ^ {2 T - 2 - i} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {L - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {R} ^ {L}\right) ^ {\frac {2 i}{L}} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {R} ^ {L}) \left(\boldsymbol {R} ^ {L}\right) ^ {2 - \frac {2 + 2 i}{L}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Since  $\pmb{R}^{L}(0) = \pmb{W}(0)$ , by existence and uniqueness theorem,  $\pmb{R}^{L}(t) = \pmb{W}(t), \forall t \in \mathbb{R}$ . So

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {M}}{\mathrm {d} t} = \boldsymbol {R} \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {R}}{d t} + \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {R}}{d t} \boldsymbol {R} = - \nabla f \left(\boldsymbol {M} ^ {L / 2}\right) \boldsymbol {M} ^ {L / 2} - \boldsymbol {M} ^ {L / 2} \nabla f \left(\boldsymbol {M} ^ {L / 2}\right),
$$

which completes the proof.

![](images/d625e8e1f186e53fd79bffa19b164a1e30a052f600e56fa97fb410c7a257241c.jpg)

# I.3 PROOF FOR THEOREM 6.2

Now we turn to prove Theorem 6.2. Let  $P = L / 2$ . Then (12) can be rewritten as

$$
\frac {d \boldsymbol {M}}{d t} = - \left(\nabla f \left(\boldsymbol {M} ^ {P}\right) \boldsymbol {M} ^ {P} + \boldsymbol {M} ^ {P} \nabla f \left(\boldsymbol {M} ^ {P}\right)\right). \tag {33}
$$

The following lemma about the growth rate of  $\lambda_{k}(M)$  is used later in the proof.

Lemma I.7. Suppose  $M(t)$  satisfies (33), we have for any  $T' > T$ , and  $k \in [d]$

$$
\lambda_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {M} \left(T ^ {\prime}\right)\right) - \lambda_ {k} (\boldsymbol {M} (T)) \leq \int_ {T} ^ {T ^ {\prime}} 2 \lambda_ {k} (\boldsymbol {M} (t)) ^ {P} \| \nabla f \left(\boldsymbol {M} ^ {P} (t)\right) \| _ {2} d t. \tag {34}
$$

and

$$
\frac {1}{P - 1} \left(\lambda_ {k} ^ {1 - P} (M (T)) - \lambda_ {k} ^ {1 - P} (M \left(T ^ {\prime}\right))\right) \leq \int_ {T} ^ {T ^ {\prime}} 2 \| \nabla f \left(M ^ {P} (t)\right) \| _ {2} d t. \tag {35}
$$

Proof. Since  $\lambda_k(M(t))$  is locally Lipschitz in  $t$ , by Rademacher's theorem, we know  $\lambda_k(M(t))$  is differentiable almost everywhere, and the following holds

$$
\lambda_ {k} (M (T ^ {\prime})) - \lambda_ {k} (M (T)) = \int_ {T} ^ {T ^ {\prime}} \frac {\mathrm {d} \lambda_ {k} (M (t))}{\mathrm {d} t} \mathrm {d} t.
$$

When  $\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda_k(M(t))}{\mathrm{d}t}$  exists, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\mathrm {d} \lambda_ {k} (\boldsymbol {M} (t))}{\mathrm {d} t} \in \left\{\left\langle \boldsymbol {G}, \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {M} (t)}{\mathrm {d} t} \right\rangle : \boldsymbol {G} \in \frac {\partial^ {\circ} \lambda_ {k} (\boldsymbol {M})}{\partial \boldsymbol {M}} \right\} \\ = \left\{2 \lambda_ {k} (\boldsymbol {M} ^ {P} (t)) \left\langle \boldsymbol {G}, - \nabla f (\boldsymbol {M} ^ {P} (t)) \right\rangle : \boldsymbol {G} \in \frac {\partial^ {\circ} \lambda_ {k} (\boldsymbol {M})}{\partial \boldsymbol {M}} \right\} \\ \end{array}
$$

Note that  $\| \pmb {G}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq \| \pmb {G}\|_{*} = 1$  . So  $|\bigl {\langle}\pmb {G}, - \nabla f(\pmb {M}^{P}(t))\bigr{\rangle}|\leq \| \nabla f(\pmb {M}^{P}(t))\|_{2}$  We can prove (35) with a similar argument.

To prove Theorem 6.2, it suffices to consider the case that  $M(0) = \hat{\alpha} I$  where  $\hat{\alpha} \coloneqq \alpha^{1 / P}$ . WLOG we can assume  $-\nabla f(\mathbf{0}) = \mathrm{diag}(\mu_1,\dots ,\mu_d)$  by choosing a suitable standard basis. By assumption in Theorem 6.2, we have  $\mu_{1} > \max \{\mu_{2},0\}$  and  $\mu_{1} = \| \nabla f(\mathbf{0})\|_{2}$ . We use  $\phi_m(M_0,t)$  to denote the solution of  $M(t)$  when  $M(0) = M_0$ .

Let  $R > 0$ . Since  $f(\cdot)$  is  $\mathcal{C}^3$ -smooth, there exists  $\beta > 0$  such that

$$
\left\| \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {1}) - \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W} _ {2}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \beta \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} - \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} \right\| _ {2}
$$

for all  $\mathbf{W}_1, \mathbf{W}_2$  with  $\| \mathbf{W}_1 \|_2, \| \mathbf{W}_2 \|_2 \leq R$ .

Let  $\kappa = \beta/\mu_1$ . We assume WLOG that  $R \leq \frac{1}{\kappa(P-1)}$ . Let  $F_{\hat{\alpha}}(x) \coloneqq \int_{x^{-(P-1)}}^{\hat{\alpha}^{-(P-1)}} \frac{dz}{1+\kappa z^{-(P-1)}}$ . Then  $F_{\hat{\alpha}}'(x) = \frac{(P-1)x^{-P}}{1+\kappa x^{P}} = \frac{P-1}{(1+\kappa x^{P})x^{P}}$ . We will use this function to bound norm growth. Let  $g_{\hat{\alpha},c}(t) = \frac{1}{\hat{\alpha}^{-(P-1)} - \kappa(P-1)c - 2\mu_{1}(P-1)t}$ . Define  $T_{\hat{\alpha}}(r) = \frac{\hat{\alpha}^{-(P-1)} - \kappa(P-1)r - r^{-(P-1)}}{2\mu_{1}(P-1)}$ . It is easy to verify that  $g_{\hat{\alpha},r}(T_{\hat{\alpha}}(r)) = r^{P-1}$ .

Lemma 1.8. For any  $x \in [\hat{\alpha}, R]$  we have

$$
\left(\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)} - x ^ {- (P - 1)}\right) - F _ {\hat {\alpha}} (x) \in [ 0, \kappa (P - 1) x ].
$$

Proof. On the one hand, we have

$$
\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)} - x ^ {- (P - 1)} - F _ {\hat {\alpha}} (x) = \int_ {x ^ {- (P - 1)}} ^ {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}} \left(1 - \frac {1}{1 + \kappa z ^ {- P / (P - 1)}}\right) d z \geq 0.
$$

On the other hand,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)} - x ^ {- (P - 1)} - F _ {\hat {\alpha}} (x) = \int_ {x ^ {- (P - 1)}} ^ {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}} \frac {\kappa}{z ^ {P / (P - 1)} + \kappa} d z \leq \kappa \int_ {x ^ {- (P - 1)}} ^ {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}} \frac {1}{z ^ {P / (P - 1)}} d z \\ = \kappa (P - 1)\cdot \frac{-1}{z^{1 / (P - 1)}}\bigg|_{x^{-(P - 1)}}^{\hat{\alpha}^{-(P - 1)}} \\ \leq \kappa (P - 1) x, \\ \end{array}
$$

which completes the proof.

![](images/786fdc9bea8e74679bd6f1f7a50854814637aeebd94cae9251babe975de51b0d.jpg)

Lemma I.9. Let  $M_0$  be a PSD matrix with  $\| M_0\| _2\leq 1$ . For  $M(t)\coloneqq \phi_{m}(\hat{\alpha} M_{0},t)$  and  $t\leq T_{\hat{\alpha}}(c)$ ,

$$
\| M (t) \| _ {2} = \lambda_ {1} (M (t)) \leq g _ {\hat {\alpha}, c} (t) ^ {\frac {1}{P - 1}}.
$$

Proof. Since  $\| \nabla f(M^P)\| _2\leq \| \nabla f(\mathbf{0})\| _2 + \beta \| M\| _2^P\leq \mu_1 + \beta (\lambda_1(M))^P$  , by Lemma I.7, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {1} (M (t)) \leq \lambda_ {1} (M (0)) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} 2 (\mu_ {1} + \beta (\lambda_ {1} (M (\tau))) ^ {P}) (\lambda_ {1} (M (\tau))) ^ {P} d \tau \\ = \hat {\alpha} + 2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1) \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {d \tau}{F _ {\hat {\alpha}} ^ {\prime} (\lambda_ {1} (\boldsymbol {M} (\tau))} \\ \end{array}
$$

So

$$
F _ {\hat {\alpha}} \left(\lambda_ {1} (M (t))\right) \leq 2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1) t.
$$

If  $\| M(t)\| _2 < \hat{\alpha}$ , then  $\| M(t)\| _2\leq g_{\hat{\alpha},c}(t)^{\frac{1}{P - 1}}$ . If  $\| M(t)\| _2\geq \hat{\alpha}$ , then by Lemma I.8,

$$
F _ {\hat {\alpha}} \left(\| M (t) \| _ {2}\right) \leq 2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1) T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (c) = \hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)} - \kappa (P - 1) c - c ^ {- (P - 1)} \leq F _ {\hat {\alpha}} (c),
$$

so  $\| M(t)\| _2\leq c$  for all  $t\leq T_{\hat{\alpha}}(c)$ . Applying Lemma I.8 again, we have

$$
\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)} - \| \boldsymbol {M} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {- (P - 1)} \leq F (\| \boldsymbol {M} (t) \| _ {2}) + \kappa (P - 1) c \leq 2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1) t + \kappa (P - 1) c,
$$

which implies  $\| M(t)\| _2\leq g_{\hat{\alpha},c}(t)^{\frac{1}{P - 1}}$  by definition.

![](images/8f5ec9084f85f3244d8d1db6835fa540f3058fef5665a2873146a6c46288b9e4.jpg)

Consider the following ODE:

$$
\frac {d \widehat {\boldsymbol {M}}}{d t} = - \left(\nabla f (\mathbf {0}) \widehat {\boldsymbol {M}} ^ {P} + \widehat {\boldsymbol {M}} ^ {P} \nabla f (\mathbf {0})\right).
$$

We use  $\hat{\phi}_m(\widehat{M}_0, t)$  to denote the solution of  $\widehat{M}(t)$  when  $\widehat{M}(0) = \widehat{M}_0$ . For diagonal matrix  $\widehat{M}_0$ ,  $\widehat{M}(t)$  is also diagonal for any  $t$ , and it is easy to show that

$$
\boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} \widehat {\boldsymbol {M}} (t) \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \left(\frac {1}{\left(\dot {\alpha} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} \widehat {\boldsymbol {M}} _ {0} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i}\right) ^ {- (P - 1)} - 2 \mu_ {i} (P - 1) t}\right) ^ {\frac {1}{P - 1}} & \quad \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} \widehat {\boldsymbol {M}} _ {0} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} \neq 0, \\ 0 & \quad \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} ^ {\top} \widehat {\boldsymbol {M}} _ {0} \boldsymbol {e} _ {i} = 0. \end{array} \right. \tag {36}
$$

Remark I.10. Unlike depth-2 case, the closed form solution,  $\widehat{\pmb{M}}(t)$  is only tractable for diagonal initialization, i.e., (36) (note that the identity matrix is diagonal). And this is the main barrier for extending our two-phase analysis to the case of general initialization when  $L \geq 3$ . In Appendix J, we give a more detailed discussion on this barrier.

The following lemma shows that the trajectory of  $M(t)$  is close to  $\widehat{M}(t)$ .

Lemma I.11. Let  $M_0$  be a diagonal PSD matrix with  $\| M_0\| _2\leq 1$ . For  $M(t)\coloneqq \phi_{m}(\hat{\alpha} M_{0},t)$  and  $\widehat{M} (t)\coloneqq \hat{\phi}_m(\hat{\alpha} M_0,t)$ , we have

$$
\left\| M \left(T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)\right) - \widehat {M} \left(T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)\right) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} = O \left(r ^ {P + 1}\right).
$$

Proof. We bound the difference  $D \coloneqq M - \widehat{M}$  between  $M$  and  $\widehat{M}$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {D}}{\mathrm {d} t} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} = 2 \left\| \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) \left(\boldsymbol {M} ^ {P} - \widehat {\boldsymbol {M}} ^ {P}\right) + \left(\nabla f (\boldsymbol {M} ^ {P}) - \nabla f (\mathbf {0})\right) \boldsymbol {M} ^ {P} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ \leq 2 \left(\| \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) \| _ {2} \| M ^ {P} - \widehat {\boldsymbol {M}} ^ {P} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} + \| \nabla f (M ^ {P}) - \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \| M ^ {P} \| _ {2}\right) \\ \leq 2 \left(\mu_ {1} P \max  \{\| M \| _ {2} ^ {P - 1}, \| \widehat {M} \| _ {2} ^ {P - 1} \} \| D \| _ {\mathrm {F}} + \beta \| M \| _ {2} ^ {2 P}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last step is by Lemma I.4. This implies that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {D} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \int_ {\tau = 0} ^ {t} \left\| \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {D} (\tau)}{\mathrm {d} \tau} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \mathrm {d} \tau \leq \int_ {0} ^ {t} 2 \left(\mu_ {1} P g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (\tau) \| \boldsymbol {D} (\tau) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} + \beta g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (\tau) ^ {\frac {2 P}{P - 1}}\right) d \tau .
$$

So

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {D} (T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)} 2 \beta g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (t) ^ {\frac {2 P}{P - 1}} \exp \left(2 \mu_ {1} P \int_ {t} ^ {T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)} g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (\tau) d \tau\right) d t \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)} 2 \beta g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (t) ^ {\frac {2 P}{P - 1}} \exp \left(\frac {P}{P - 1} \ln \frac {g _ {\hat {\alpha} , r} \left(T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)\right)}{g _ {\hat {\alpha} , r} (t)}\right) d t \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)} 2 \beta g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (t) ^ {\frac {P}{P - 1}} g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} \left(T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)\right) ^ {\frac {P}{P - 1}} d t \\ = 2 \beta \cdot \frac {1}{2 \mu_ {1}} g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)) ^ {\frac {1}{P - 1}} \cdot g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)) ^ {\frac {P}{P - 1}} \\ = \kappa g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} \left(T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)\right) ^ {\frac {P + 1}{P - 1}} \\ = \kappa r ^ {P + 1}. \\ \end{array}
$$

which proves the bound.

![](images/5d6f8d4e0858d711ad5eaf51eaad0948714ce3869fc2144445c4ef75ec2e420f.jpg)

Lemma 1.12. Let  $M(t) = \phi_m(\hat{\alpha} M_0, t)$ ,  $\widetilde{M}(t) = \phi_m(\hat{\alpha}\widetilde{M}_0, t)$ . If  $\max\{\|M_0\|_2, \| \widetilde{M}_0\|_2\} \leq 1$ . For  $t \leq T_{\hat{\alpha}}(r)$ , we have

$$
\| \boldsymbol {M} (t) - \widetilde {\boldsymbol {M}} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \left(\frac {r}{\hat {\alpha}}\right) ^ {P} e ^ {2 \kappa r ^ {P}} \| \boldsymbol {M} (0) - \widetilde {\boldsymbol {M}} (0) \| _ {\mathrm {F}}.
$$

Proof. Define  $D(t) = M(t) - \widetilde{M}(t)$ . Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \frac {\mathrm {d} D}{\mathrm {d} t} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} = 2 \left\| \left(\nabla f (M ^ {P}) \left(M ^ {P} - \widetilde {M} ^ {P}\right) + \left(\nabla f (M ^ {P}) - \nabla f (\widetilde {M} ^ {P})\right) \widetilde {M} ^ {P}\right) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ \leq 2 \left(\| \nabla f (\boldsymbol {M} ^ {P}) \| _ {2} \| \boldsymbol {M} ^ {P} - \widetilde {\boldsymbol {M}} ^ {P} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} + \beta \| \boldsymbol {M} ^ {P} - \widetilde {\boldsymbol {M}} ^ {P} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {M}} ^ {P} \| _ {2}\right) \\ \leq 2 \left(\mu_ {1} + \beta \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {M}} \| _ {2} ^ {P} + \beta \| \boldsymbol {M} \| _ {2} ^ {P}\right) P \max  \{\| \boldsymbol {M} \| _ {2} ^ {P - 1}, \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {M}} \| _ {2} ^ {P - 1} \} \| \boldsymbol {D} \| _ {\mathrm {F}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last step is by Lemma I.4. So

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {D} (T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \| \boldsymbol {D} (0) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \cdot \exp \left(2 P \mu_ {1} \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)} \left(1 + 2 \kappa g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (t) ^ {\frac {P}{P - 1}}\right) g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (t) d t\right) \\ \leq \| \boldsymbol {D} (0) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \cdot \exp \left(\frac {P}{P - 1} \ln \frac {g _ {\hat {\alpha} , r} (T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r))}{g _ {\hat {\alpha} , r} (0)} + 2 \kappa g _ {\hat {\alpha}, r} (T _ {\hat {\alpha}} (r)) ^ {\frac {P}{P - 1}}\right) \\ \leq \| \boldsymbol {D} (0) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \left(\frac {r}{\hat {\alpha}}\right) ^ {P} e ^ {2 \kappa r ^ {P}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

which proves the bound.

![](images/3b0d8cfbcd9e6e09a4cb62e190bfd76943c5a99497feebfe869641b4c6775314.jpg)

Let  $M_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}}(t)\coloneqq \phi_m\left(\alpha \pmb {e}_1\pmb {e}_1^\top ,\frac{\hat{\alpha}^{-(P - 1)}}{2\mu_1(P - 1)} +t\right)$ . Let  $\overline{M} (t)\coloneqq \lim_{\alpha \to 0}M_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{G}}(t)$

Lemma I.13. For every  $t \in (-\infty, +\infty)$ ,  $\overline{M}(t)$  exists and  $M_{\hat{\alpha}}^{\mathrm{G}}(t)$  converges to  $\overline{M}(t)$  in the following rate:

$$
\left\| M _ {\hat {\alpha}} ^ {G} (t) - \overline {{M}} (t) \right\| _ {F} = O (\hat {\alpha}).
$$

Proof. Let  $c$  be a sufficiently small constant. Let  $\bar{T} \coloneqq \frac{-\kappa(P - 1)c - c^{-(P - 1)}}{2\mu_1(P - 1)}$ . We prove this lemma in the cases of  $t \in (-\infty, \bar{T}]$  and  $t > \bar{T}$  respectively.

Case 1. Fix  $t \in (-\infty, \bar{T}]$ . Then  $\frac{\hat{\alpha}^{-(P-1)}}{2\mu_1(P-1)} + t \leq T_{\hat{\alpha}}(c)$ . Let  $\tilde{\alpha}$  be the unique number such that  $\kappa(P-1)\tilde{\alpha} + \tilde{\alpha}^{-(P-1)} = \hat{\alpha}^{-(P-1)}$ . Let  $\hat{\alpha}' < \hat{\alpha}$  be an arbitrarily small number. Let  $t_0 := T_{\hat{\alpha}'}(\tilde{\alpha}) = \frac{(\hat{\alpha}')^{-(P-1)} - \hat{\alpha}^{-(P-1)}}{2\mu_1(P-1)}$ . By Lemma I.11 and (36), we have

$$
\left\| \phi_ {m} (\hat {\alpha} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top}, t _ {0}) - \hat {\alpha} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \left\| \phi_ {m} (\hat {\alpha} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top}, t _ {0}) - \hat {\phi} _ {m} (\hat {\alpha} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top}, t _ {0}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq O (\tilde {\alpha} ^ {P + 1}).
$$

By Lemma I.9,  $\| \phi_{m}(\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}\pmb{e}_{1}\pmb{e}_{1}^{\top},t_{0})\|_{2}\leq \tilde{\alpha}$ . Then by Lemma I.12, we have

$$
\left\| \phi_ {m} (\hat {\alpha} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top}, t _ {0} + t) - \phi (\hat {\alpha} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top}, t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \left(\frac {c}{\tilde {\alpha}}\right) ^ {P} e ^ {2 \kappa c ^ {P}} \cdot O (\tilde {\alpha} ^ {P + 1}) = O (\tilde {\alpha}) = O (\hat {\alpha}).
$$

This implies that  $\{M_{\hat{\alpha}}^{\mathrm{G}}(t)\}$  satisfies Cauchy's criterion for every  $t$ , and thus the limit  $\overline{M}(t)$  exists for  $t \leq \bar{T}$ . The convergence rate can be deduced by taking limits for  $\hat{\alpha}' \to 0$  on both sides.

Case 2. For  $t = \bar{T} +\tau$  with  $\tau >0,\phi_m(M,\tau)$  is locally Lipschitz with respect to  $M$ . So

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (t) - \boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha} ^ {\prime}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} = \left\| \phi_ {m} (\boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (\bar {T}), \tau) - \phi_ {m} (\boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha} ^ {\prime}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (\bar {T}), \tau) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ = O \big (\left\| M _ {\hat {\alpha}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (\bar {T}) - M _ {\hat {\alpha} ^ {\prime}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (\bar {T}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \big) \\ = O (\hat {\alpha}), \\ \end{array}
$$

which proves the lemma for  $t > \bar{T}$

![](images/020031962a09cce9154a6a3d4bd9a623b8ced00455dce245afd95a48f464d38e.jpg)

Theorem I.14. For every  $t \in (-\infty, +\infty)$ , as  $\alpha \to 0$ , we have:

$$
\left\| \phi_ {m} \left(\hat {\alpha} I, \frac {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right) - \overline {{\boldsymbol {M}}} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} = O \left(\hat {\alpha} ^ {\frac {1}{P + 1}}\right), \tag {37}
$$

and for any  $2 \leq k \leq d$ ,

$$
\lambda_ {k} \left(\phi_ {m} \left(\hat {\alpha} I, \frac {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right)\right) = O (\hat {\alpha}). \tag {38}
$$

Proof. Let  $M_{\hat{\alpha}}(t) \coloneqq \phi_m\left(\hat{\alpha} I, \frac{\hat{\alpha}^{-(P-1)}}{2\mu_1(P-1)} + t\right)$ . Again we let  $c$  be a sufficiently small constant and  $\bar{T} \coloneqq \frac{-\kappa(P-1)c - c^{-(P-1)}}{2\mu_1(P-1)}$ . We prove in the cases of  $t \in (-\infty, \bar{T}]$  and  $t > \bar{T}$  respectively.

Case 1. Fix  $t \in (-\infty, \bar{T}]$ . Let  $\hat{\alpha}_1 \coloneqq \hat{\alpha}^{\frac{1}{P + 1}}$ . Let  $\tilde{\alpha}_1$  be the unique number such that  $\kappa(P - 1)\tilde{\alpha}_1 + \tilde{\alpha}_1^{-(P - 1)} = \hat{\alpha}_1^{-(P - 1)}$ . Let  $t_0 \coloneqq T_{\hat{\alpha}}(\tilde{\alpha}_1) = \frac{\hat{\alpha}^{-(P - 1)} - \hat{\alpha}_1^{-(P - 1)}}{2\mu_1(P - 1)}$ . Then

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \phi_ {m} (\hat {\alpha} \boldsymbol {I}, t _ {0}) - \hat {\alpha} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \left\| \phi_ {m} (\hat {\alpha} \boldsymbol {I}, t _ {0}) - \hat {\phi} _ {m} (\hat {\alpha} \boldsymbol {I}, t _ {0}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} + \left\| \hat {\phi} _ {m} (\hat {\alpha} \boldsymbol {I}, t _ {0}) - \hat {\alpha} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ = O \left(\tilde {\alpha} _ {1} ^ {P + 1} + \hat {\alpha}\right) \\ = O (\hat {\alpha}). \\ \end{array}
$$

By Lemma I.9,  $\| \phi_{m}(\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}\pmb{I},t_{0})\|_{2}\leq \tilde{\alpha}_{1}$ . Then by Lemma I.12, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha}} (t) - \boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha} _ {1}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} = \left\| \phi_ {m} (\hat {\alpha} \boldsymbol {I}, t _ {0} + t) - \phi_ {m} (\hat {\alpha} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} \boldsymbol {e} _ {1} ^ {\top}, t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ \leq \left(\frac {c}{\tilde {\alpha} _ {1}}\right) ^ {P} e ^ {2 \kappa c ^ {P}} \cdot O (\hat {\alpha}) = O (\hat {\alpha} ^ {\frac {1}{P + 1}}). \\ \end{array}
$$

Combining this with the convergence rate for  $M_{\hat{\alpha}_1}^{\mathrm{G}}(t)$  proves the bound (37).

For (38), by Lemma I.7, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {k} ^ {1 - P} \left(M _ {\hat {\alpha}} (\bar {T})\right) - \lambda_ {k} ^ {1 - P} \left(M _ {\hat {\alpha}} (t _ {0})\right) \leq \int_ {t _ {0}} ^ {\bar {T}} 2 (P - 1) \left\| \nabla f \left(M _ {\hat {\alpha}} ^ {P} (t)\right) \right\| _ {2} d t \\ \leq \int_ {t _ {0}} ^ {\bar {T}} 2 (P - 1) \left(\mu_ {1} + \beta \| M _ {\hat {\alpha}} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {P}\right)) d t \tag {39} \\ \leq - 2 (P - 1) \left(\mu_ {1} (t - T _ {1}) + \frac {\kappa}{2} \cdot g _ {\hat {\alpha}, c} (t) ^ {\frac {1}{P - 1}}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

By Lemma I.11,  $\lambda_1(M_{\hat{\alpha}}(\bar{T})) = \left\| M_{\hat{\alpha}}(\bar{T})\right\| _2 = c + O(c^{P + 1})$  . For  $k\geq 2$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {k} \left(M _ {\hat {\alpha}} (\bar {T})\right) ^ {- (P - 1)} \geq \Omega \left(\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}\right) - 2 (P - 1) \left(\mu_ {1} (\bar {T} - T _ {1}) + \frac {\kappa}{2} \cdot c\right) \\ \geq \Omega (\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}) - \frac {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- \frac {P - 1}{P + 1}} - c ^ {- (P - 1)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} - O (c) \\ \geq \Omega (\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}). \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus  $\lambda_{k}(M_{\hat{\alpha}}(\bar{T}))\leq O(\hat{\alpha})$

Case 2. For  $t = \bar{T} +\tau$  with  $\tau >0,\phi_m(M,\tau)$  is locally Lipschitz with respect to  $M$  . So

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha}} (t) - \boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha} _ {1}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} = \left\| \phi_ {m} (\boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha}} (\bar {T}), \tau) - \phi_ {m} (\boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha} _ {1}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (\bar {T}), \tau) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ = O \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha}} (\bar {T}) - \boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha} _ {1}} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (\bar {T}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}}\right) = O \bigl (\hat {\alpha} ^ {\frac {1}{P + 1}} \bigr), \\ \end{array}
$$

which proves the bound (37).

For (38), again by Lemma I.7, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {k} ^ {1 - P} \left(M _ {\hat {\alpha}} (\bar {T})\right) - \lambda_ {k} ^ {1 - P} \left(M _ {\hat {\alpha}} (\bar {T} + \tau)\right) \\ \leq \int_ {\bar {T}} ^ {\bar {T} + \tau} 2 (P - 1) \left\| \nabla f (\boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha}} ^ {P} (t)) \right\| _ {2} \mathrm {d} t \\ \leq \int_ {\bar {T}} ^ {\bar {T} + \tau} 2 (P - 1) \left(\beta \left\| \boldsymbol {M} _ {\hat {\alpha}} ^ {P} (t) - (\boldsymbol {M} ^ {\mathrm {G}}) ^ {P} (t) \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \nabla f \left((\boldsymbol {M} ^ {\mathrm {G}}) ^ {P} (t)\right) \right\| _ {2}\right) \mathrm {d} t \\ \leq \int_ {\bar {T}} ^ {\bar {T} + \tau} 2 (P - 1) \left(O \left(\hat {\alpha} ^ {\frac {1}{1 + P}}\right) + \beta \left\| \boldsymbol {M} ^ {\mathrm {G}} (t) \right\| _ {2} ^ {P}\right) \mathrm {d} t \\ \leq O (1). \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus  $\lambda_k^{1 - P}(M_{\hat{\alpha}}(\bar{T} + \tau)) = \Omega(\hat{\alpha}^{-(P - 1)})$ , that is,  $\lambda_k(M_{\hat{\alpha}}(\bar{T} + \tau)) = O(\hat{\alpha}), \forall k \geq 2$ .

Proof of Theorem 6.2. Note that  $\left(\overline{\boldsymbol{M}}(t)\right)^P = \overline{\boldsymbol{W}}(t)$  and

$$
\left(\phi_ {m} \left(\hat {\alpha} \boldsymbol {I}, \frac {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right)\right) ^ {P} = \phi \left(\alpha \boldsymbol {I}, \frac {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right).
$$

By Theorem I.14, We have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \phi \left(\alpha I, \frac {\alpha^ {- (1 - 1 / P)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right) - \overline {{\boldsymbol {W}}} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ \leq \left\| \left(\phi_ {m} \left(\hat {\alpha} \boldsymbol {I}, \frac {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right)\right) ^ {P} - \left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {M}}} (t)\right) ^ {P} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ \leq P \left\| \phi_ {m} \left(\hat {\alpha} I, \frac {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right) - \overline {{\boldsymbol {M}}} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \max  \left(\left\| \phi_ {m} \left(\hat {\alpha} I, \frac {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right) \right\| _ {2}, \left\| \overline {{\boldsymbol {M}}} (t) \right\| _ {2}\right) ^ {P - 1} \\ = O (\hat {\alpha} ^ {\frac {1}{P + 1}}) O (1) = O \left(\alpha^ {\frac {1}{P (P + 1)}}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

and for  $2 \leq k \leq d$ ,

$$
\lambda_ {k} \left(\phi \left(\alpha {\pmb I}, \frac {\alpha^ {- (1 - 1 / P)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right)\right) = \lambda_ {k} \left(\phi_ {m} \left(\hat {\alpha} {\pmb I}, \frac {\hat {\alpha} ^ {- (P - 1)}}{2 \mu_ {1} (P - 1)} + t\right)\right) = O (\hat {\alpha} ^ {P}) = O (\alpha).
$$

![](images/373fcba3866fc98f41af3a11212abffede72e824ac4aea3dc876ebdb8d3cfe5d.jpg)

# J ESCAPING DIRECTION FOR DEEP MATRIX FACTORIZATION

For deep matrix factorization, recall that we only prove that GF with infinitesimal identity initialization escapes in the direction of the top eigenvector. The main burden for us to generalize this proof to general initialization is that we don't know how to analyze the early phase dynamics of (12), i.e., the analytical solution of (40) is difficult to compute, when  $L \geq 3$ . Intuitively, the direction that the infinitesimal initialization escapes 0 is exactly  $\overline{M} := \lim_{t \to \infty} \frac{M(t)}{\|M(t)\|_F}$ , where  $M(t)$  is the solution of (40). Showing  $\overline{M} = v_1 v_1^\top$  is a critical step in our analysis towards convergence to GLRL.

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {M}}{\mathrm {d} t} = - \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) \boldsymbol {M} ^ {L / 2} - \boldsymbol {M} ^ {L / 2} \nabla f (\mathbf {0}). \tag {40}
$$

However, unlike the depth-2 case,  $\overline{M}$  can be different from  $v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}$  even if  $v_{1}^{\top}M(0)v_{1} > 0$ . We here give an example for diagonal  $M(0)$  and  $\nabla f(0)$  at Appendix J.2. Nevertheless, we still conjecture that except for a zero measure set of  $M(0)$ ,  $\overline{M} = v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}$ , based on the following theoretical and experimental evidences:

- If  $\pmb{v}_1^\top \pmb{M}(0)\pmb{v}_1 > 0$  and  $\mathrm{rank}(M(0)) = 1$ , we prove that  $\overline{\pmb{M}} = \pmb{v}_1\pmb{v}_1^\top$ . (See Theorem J.1)  
- For the counter-example, we show experimentally, even with perturbation of only magnitude  $10^{-5}$ ,  $\overline{M} = v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}$ . The results are shown at Figure 7. The  $y$ -axis indicates  $\langle v_{1}, u_{1}(t) \rangle$  where  $u_{1}(t)$  is the top eigenvector of  $M(t)$ . As  $\|W(t)\|_{\mathrm{F}}$  becomes larger,  $u_{1}(t)$  aligns better with  $v_{1}$ , which means the noise helps  $M$  escaping from  $v_{1}$ . The larger the noise is, the faster  $u_{1}(t)$  converges to  $v_{1}$ .

# J.1 RANK-ONE CASE

Theorem J.1 (rank-1 initialization escapes along the top eigenvector). When  $\mathrm{rank}(M(0)) = 1$ ,  $\lim_{t\to \infty}\frac{M(t)}{\|M(t)\|_F} = \pmb {v}_1\pmb {v}_1^\top$  if  $\pmb {v}_1^\top M(0)\pmb {v}_1 > 0$ .

Proof. Let  $\pmb{u}(0)$  be the vector such that  $M(0) = \pmb{u}(0)\pmb{u}(0)^{\top}$  and  $\pmb{u}(t) \in \mathbb{R}^d$  be the solution of

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {u} (t)}{\mathrm {d} t} = \| \boldsymbol {u} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {L - 2} \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) \boldsymbol {u} (t).
$$

It is easy to check that  $\pmb{M}(t) = \pmb{u}(t)\pmb{u}(t)^{\top}$  is the solution of (40), because

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {M}}{\mathrm {d} t} = \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {u}}{\mathrm {d} t} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {u} \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {u}}{\mathrm {d} t} ^ {\top} = - \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) \boldsymbol {M} (t) \| \boldsymbol {u} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {L - 2} - \boldsymbol {M} (t) \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) \| \boldsymbol {u} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {L - 2} \\ = - \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) M ^ {L / 2} - M ^ {L / 2} \nabla f (\mathbf {0}). \\ \end{array}
$$

Let  $\tau(t) = \int_0^t \| \pmb{u}(s) \|_2^{L-2} \, \mathrm{d}s$ . Then

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {u}}{\mathrm {d} \tau} = \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {u}}{\mathrm {d} t} \frac {\mathrm {d} t}{\mathrm {d} \tau} = - \frac {1}{\frac {\mathrm {d} \tau}{\mathrm {d} t}} \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2} ^ {L - 2} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {0}) \boldsymbol {u} = - \nabla f (\boldsymbol {0}) \boldsymbol {u}.
$$

That is, under time rescaling  $t \to \tau(t)$ , the trajectory of  $\pmb{u}(t)$  still follows the power iteration, regardless of the depth  $L$ .

# J.2 COUNTER-EXAMPLE FOR ESCAPING DIRECTION

Let  $\nabla f(\mathbf{0}) = \mathrm{diag}(2,0.9,0.8,\ldots ,0.1)\in \mathbb{R}^{10\times 10}$  be diagonal. Let  $\pmb {W}(0)$  be also diagonal and  $\pmb {W}(0)_{i,i}\sim \mathrm{Unif}[0.9,1.1]\cdot \alpha$  for  $i\in [10]\setminus \{2\}$ ,  $\pmb {W}(0)_{2,2} = 16\alpha$ , where  $\alpha = 10^{-16}$  is a small constant. Let the depth be 4.

Lemma J.2. With  $\nabla f(\mathbf{0})$  and  $W(0)$  constructed above,  $\pmb{v}_1\pmb{M}(0)\pmb{v}_1^\top > 0$  and  $\overline{M} \neq \pmb{v}_1\pmb{v}_1^\top$ .

Proof. It is easy to check that  $\pmb{v}_1 = \pmb{e}_1$ , so  $\pmb{v}_1\pmb{M}(0)\pmb{v}_1^\top > 0$ . Now we prove that  $\overline{M}(\infty) \neq \pmb{v}_1\pmb{v}_1^\top$ .

As both  $\pmb{W}(0)$  and  $\nabla f(\mathbf{0})$  are diagonal,  $\pmb{W}(t)$  is always diagonal and has dynamics

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} M (t) _ {i , i}}{\mathrm {d} t} = - 2 \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) _ {i, i} M (t) _ {i, i} ^ {2}, \quad \forall i \in [ 1 0 ],
$$

therefore we have closed form of  $M(t)$ :

$$
M (t) _ {i, i} ^ {- 1} = M (0) _ {i, i} ^ {- 1} - 2 \nabla f (\mathbf {0}) _ {i, i} t, \quad \forall i \in [ 1 0 ].
$$

For  $i \in [10]$ , the time for  $M(t)_{i,i}$  going to infinity is  $(2M(0)_{i,i} \nabla f(\mathbf{0})_{i,i})^{-1}$ . By simple calculation,  $M(t)_{2,2}$  goes to infinity the fastest, thus  $\overline{M} = \pmb{e}_2 \pmb{e}_2^\top \neq \pmb{v}_1 \pmb{v}_1^\top$ .

![](images/98384729789d9b2dff6f9dc685dc23b649076ab1638553f182e4f86bcc362225.jpg)  
Figure 7: Dynamics of  $\frac{\mathrm{d}M}{\mathrm{d}t} = -\nabla f(\mathbf{0})M^{L / 2} - M^{L / 2}\nabla f(\mathbf{0})$  plotted, where  $L = 4$ ,  $\pmb{u}_1(t)$  is the top eigenvector of  $\pmb{W}(t)$  and  $\epsilon$  is the relative magnitude of noise. The initialization we use in this experiment is  $\pmb{W}_{\mathrm{noise}}(0) = \pmb{W}(0) + \frac{\alpha\epsilon}{2} (\pmb{Z} + \pmb{Z}^{\top})$ , where  $\pmb{W}(0)$  is what we construct at Appendix J.2, and  $\pmb{Z}$  is a matrix where entries are i.i.d. samples from the standard Gaussian distribution  $\mathcal{N}(0,1)$ . We run 5 fixed random seeds (the noise matrix) for each  $\epsilon$ . The trajectory of  $\pmb{W}$  is calculated by simulating gradient flow on  $\pmb{M}$  with small timestep and RMSprop (Tieleman and Hinton, 2012) for faster convergence.

We remark that the scales of  $W(0)$  and  $\nabla f(\mathbf{0})$  do not matter as in gradient flow, as scaling  $\nabla f(\mathbf{0})$  is equivalent to scaling time (by Lemma J.3 below). And for this reason, the  $x$ -axis is the chosen as  $\frac{\|W(t)\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\|W(0)\|_{\mathrm{F}}}$ , the relative growth rate.

Lemma J.3. Suppose  $\pmb{g}:\mathbb{R}^d\to \mathbb{R}^d$  is a  $P$ -homogeneous function, that is,  $\pmb {g}(\alpha \pmb {\theta}) = \lambda^{P}\pmb {g}(\alpha)$  for any  $\alpha >0$ , and  $\frac{\mathrm{d}\pmb{\theta}'(t)}{\mathrm{d}t} = \pmb {g}(\pmb {\theta}'(t))$ . Then  $\alpha \pmb{\theta}'(\alpha^{P - 1}t)$  is the solution of

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {\theta} (t)}{\mathrm {d} t} = \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)), \quad \boldsymbol {\theta} (0) = \alpha \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime} (0). \tag {41}
$$

Proof. Simply plug in  $\pmb{\theta}(t) = \alpha \pmb{\theta}'(\alpha^{P-1}t)$ , then we have

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {\theta} (t)}{\mathrm {d} t} = \frac {\mathrm {d} \alpha \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime} \left(\alpha^ {P - 1} t\right)}{\mathrm {d} t} = \alpha^ {P} \frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime} \left(\alpha^ {P - 1} t\right)}{\mathrm {d} \left(\alpha^ {P - 1} t\right)} = \alpha^ {P} \boldsymbol {g} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime} \left(\alpha^ {P - 1} t\right)\right) = \boldsymbol {g} \left(\alpha \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime} \left(\alpha^ {P - 1} t\right)\right) = \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {\theta} (t)).
$$

![](images/adeeeda4095de46f1c36744a6b176a86e6f6af581a140f58e8171e2d659ae9e5.jpg)

# K PROOF OF LINEAR CONVERGENCE TO MINIMIZER

In this section, we will present the theorems that guarantee the linear convergence to a minimizer  $\mathbf{W}_0$  of  $f(\cdot)$  if the dynamics (42) is initialized sufficiently close to  $\mathbf{W}_0$ , i.e.,  $\| \mathbf{W}(0) - \mathbf{W}_0 \|_{\mathrm{F}}$  is sufficiently small. In Appendix K.3, we will apply this result to prove Theorem 6.4.

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {W}}{\mathrm {d} t} = - \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {L - 1} \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\frac {2 i}{L}} \nabla f (\boldsymbol {W}) \boldsymbol {W} ^ {2 - \frac {2 i + 2}{L}} =: \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {W}). \tag {42}
$$

Throughout this section, we assume  $\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{W}_0) = k$  and use  $m\coloneqq \lambda_k(\mathbf{W}_0)$  to denote the  $k$ -th smallest non-zero eigenvalue of  $\mathbf{W}_0$ . The tangent space of manifold of rank-  $k$  symmetric matrices at  $\mathbf{W}_0$  is  $\mathcal{T} = \{\mathbf{V}\mathbf{W}_0^\top +\mathbf{W}_0\mathbf{V}^\top :\mathbf{V}\in \mathbb{R}^{d\times k}\}$ . It can be shown that  $\dim (\mathcal{T}) = k(d - k) + \frac{k(k + 1)}{2} = \frac{k(2d - k + 1)}{2}$ .

Let  $J(\mathbf{W})$  be the Jacobian of  $g(\mathbf{W})$  in (42). For depth-2 case, we have shown that  $\mathcal{T}$  is an invariant subspace of  $J(W_0)$  in Theorem F.5, property 2. This can be generalize to the deep case where  $L \geq 3$ . Therefore, we can use  $J(W_0)|_{\mathcal{T}} \colon \mathcal{T} \to \mathcal{T}$  to denote the linear operator  $J(W_0)$  restricted on  $\mathcal{T}$ . We also define  $\Pi_1^{d^2}(\mathbf{W})$  as the projection of  $\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$  on  $\mathcal{T}$ , and  $\Pi_2^{d^2}(\mathbf{W}) \coloneqq \mathbf{W} - \Pi_1^{d^2}(\mathbf{W})$ .

Towards showing the main convergence result in the section, we make the following assumption.

Assumption K.1. Suppose  $J(W_0)|_{\mathcal{T}}$  diagonalizable and all eigenvalues are negative real numbers.

$\pmb{W}_{0}$  is a minimizer, so it is clear that  $\pmb {J}(\pmb {W}_0)|_{\mathcal{T}}$  has no eigenvalues with positive real parts (otherwise there is a descending direction of  $f(\cdot)$  from  $\pmb{W}_0$ , since the loss  $f(\cdot)$  strictly decreases along the trajectory of (42)). If further Assumption K.1 holds, then we know  $\pmb {J}(\pmb {W}_0)|_{\mathcal{T}}\colon \mathcal{T}\to \mathcal{T}$  can be diagonalized as  $\pmb {J}(\pmb {W}_0)|_{\mathcal{T}}[\cdot ] = \mathcal{V}(\Sigma \mathcal{V}^{-1}(\cdot))$ , where  $\pmb {\Sigma}_i = \mathrm{diag}(-\mu_1,\dots , - \mu_{\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{T})})$ ,  $\mathcal{V}$ :  $\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{T})}\rightarrow \mathcal{T}$ ,  $\mathcal{V}(\pmb {x}) = \sum_{i = 1}^{\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{T})}x_iV_i$ , and  $\pmb {V}_i$  is the eigenvector associated with eigenvalue  $-\mu_{i}$ .

As shown in Theorem K.3 below, this assumption implies that if  $\mathbf{W}(0)$  is rank-  $k$  and is sufficiently close to  $\mathbf{W}_0$ , then  $\| \mathbf{W}(t) - \mathbf{W}_0 \|_F \leq Ce^{-\mu_1 t}$  for some constant  $C$ . For depth-2 case, the above assumption is equivalent to that  $\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{U}_0)$  is "strongly convex" at  $\mathbf{U}_0$ , except those 0 eigenvalues due to symmetry, by property 2 of Theorem F.5). For the case where  $L \geq 3$ , because this dynamics is not gradient flow, in general it does not correspond to a loss function and strongly convexity does not make any sense. Nevertheless, in experiments we do observe linear convergence to  $\mathbf{W}_0$ , so this assumption is reasonable.

# K.1 RANK-  $k$  INITIALIZATION

For convenience, we define for all  $\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{S}_d$ ,

$$
\| \boldsymbol {W}\|_{\mathcal{V}}:= \left\| \mathcal{V}^{-1}\left(\Pi_{1}^{d^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol {W}\right)\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}},\quad \| \boldsymbol {W}\|_{\mathrm{F},1}:= \left\| \Pi_{1}^{d^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol {W}\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}},\quad \| \boldsymbol {W}\|_{\mathrm{F},2}:= \left\| \Pi_{2}^{d^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol {W}\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}.
$$

The reason for such definition of norms, as we will see later, is that the norm (or the difference) in the tangent space of the manifold of symmetric rank- $r$  matrices,  $\| \boldsymbol{W} - \boldsymbol{W}'\|_{\mathrm{F},1}$ , dominates that in the orthogonal complement of the tangent space,  $\| \boldsymbol{W} - \boldsymbol{W}'\|_{\mathrm{F},2}$ , when both  $\boldsymbol{W}$ ,  $\boldsymbol{W}'$  get very close to the  $W_0$  (see a more rigorous statement in Lemma K.2). WLOG, we can assume

$$
\frac {\| \cdot \| _ {F , 1}}{K} \leq \| \cdot \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \leq \| \cdot \| _ {F, 1},
$$

for some constant  $K$ , which may depend on  $f$  and  $\mathbf{W}_0$ . This also implies that  $\| \cdot \|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq \| \cdot \|_{\mathrm{F}}$ . Below we also assume for sufficiently small  $R$ , and any  $\mathbf{W}$  such that  $\| \mathbf{W} - \mathbf{W}_0 \|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq R$ , we have  $\| \nabla f(\mathbf{W}) \|_2 \leq \rho$  and  $\| \mathbf{J}(\mathbf{W}) [\Delta] \|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq \beta \| \Delta \|_{\mathrm{F}}$  for any  $\Delta$ . In the proof below, we assume such properties hold as long as we can show the boundedness of  $\mathbf{W}(t) - \mathbf{W}_0$ .

Lemma K.2. Let  $\max \{\| \pmb{W} - \pmb{W}_0\|_{\mathrm{F},1},\| \pmb{W}' - \pmb{W}_0\|_{\mathrm{F},1}\} = r$  when  $r\leq \frac{m}{2}$ , we have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {W} - \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\prime} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \leq \frac {5 r}{m} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} - \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\prime} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1}.
$$

As a special case, we have

$$
\| \boldsymbol {W} - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \leq \frac {5 \| \boldsymbol {W} - \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\prime} \| _ {\mathrm {F} , 1} ^ {2}}{m}.
$$

Proof. WLOG we can assume  $\mathbf{W}_0$  is only non-zero in the first  $k$  dimension, i.e.,  $[\mathbf{W}_0]_{ij} = 0$ , for all  $i \geq k + 1$ ,  $j \geq k + 1$ . We further denote  $\mathbf{W}$  and  $\mathbf{W}'$  by

$$
\boldsymbol {W} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {A} & \boldsymbol {B} ^ {\top} \\ \boldsymbol {B} & \boldsymbol {C} \end{array} \right] \quad \text {a n d} \quad \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\prime} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {A} ^ {\prime} & \boldsymbol {B} ^ {\prime \top} \\ \boldsymbol {B} ^ {\prime} & \boldsymbol {C} ^ {\prime} \end{array} \right],
$$

where  $\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\in \mathbb{R}^{k\times k},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\in \mathbb{R}^{(d - k)\times k},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in \mathbb{R}^{(d - k)\times (d - k)}$ . By definition, we have  $\| \mathbf{A} - \mathbf{A}'\| _F,\| \mathbf{B} - \mathbf{B}'\| _F\leq \| \mathbf{W} - \mathbf{W}'\|_{\mathrm{F},1}$  and  $\| \pmb {W} - \pmb {W}'\|_{\mathrm{F},2} = \| \pmb {C} - \pmb {C}'\| _F$ . Moreover, we have  $\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{A})\geq m - \| \mathbf{A} - \mathbf{W}_0\| _F\geq m - \| \mathbf{W} - \mathbf{W}_0\|_{\mathrm{F},1}\geq \frac{m}{2}$ .

Since  $W, W'$  is rank- $k$ , we have  $C = BA^{-1}B^{\top}$ ,  $C' = B'A'^{-1}B'^{\top}$ . Thus

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} - \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\prime} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \\ = \| \boldsymbol {C} - \boldsymbol {C} ^ {\prime} \| _ {F} \\ = \left\| \boldsymbol {B} \boldsymbol {A} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {B} ^ {\top} - \boldsymbol {B} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {A} ^ {\prime - 1} \boldsymbol {B} ^ {\prime} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {F} \\ \leq \| B - B ^ {\prime} \| _ {F} \| A ^ {- 1} B ^ {\top} \| _ {F} + \| B A ^ {- 1} \| _ {F} \| A ^ {\prime} - A \| _ {F} \| A ^ {\prime - 1} B ^ {\prime \top} \| _ {F} + \| B ^ {\prime} A ^ {\prime - 1} \| _ {F} \| B ^ {\top} - B ^ {\prime \top} \| _ {F} \\ \leq \| \boldsymbol {W} - \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\prime} \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1} \frac {2 r}{m} + \| \boldsymbol {W} - \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\prime} \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1} \left(\frac {2 r}{m}\right) ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {W} - \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\prime} \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1} \frac {2 r}{m} \\ \leq \| \boldsymbol {W} - \boldsymbol {W} ^ {\prime} \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1} \frac {5 r}{m}. \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/3419aced53d8064e77c1ead4cdfdbdd2f20cb0c9e93e88b82138296514a23a75.jpg)

Theorem K.3 (Linear convergence of rank- $k$  matrices). Suppose that  $\operatorname{rank}(\mathbf{W}(0)) = \operatorname{rank}(\mathbf{W}_0) = k$  and

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {W} (0) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \leq R := \max  \left\{\frac {m}{2 K}, \frac {\mu_ {1}}{K ^ {2} (2 9 \beta + 1 0 \rho / m)} \right\},
$$

we have  $\| \pmb{W}(t) - \pmb{W}_0\|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq Ce^{-\mu_1 t} \| \pmb{W}(0) - \pmb{W}_0\|_{\mathcal{V}}$  for some constant  $C$  depending on  $\pmb{W}_0$ , where  $\pmb{W}(t)$  satisfies (42).

Proof. For convenience, we define  $\mathbf{W}_1(t) \coloneqq \Pi_1^{d^2}(\mathbf{W}(t) - \mathbf{W}_0), \mathbf{W}_2(t) \coloneqq \Pi_2^{d^2}(\mathbf{W}(t) - \mathbf{W}_0) = \Pi_2^{d^2}(\mathbf{W}(t))$ . We also use  $\langle \cdot, \cdot \rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{-1}} = \langle \mathcal{V}^{-1}(\cdot), \mathcal{V}^{-1}(\cdot) \rangle$  for short.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\mathrm {d} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2}}{\mathrm {d} t} = \frac {\mathrm {d} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2}}{\mathrm {d} t} \\ = 2 \left\langle \Pi_ {1} ^ {d ^ {2}} \left(\frac {\mathrm {d} \boldsymbol {W} (t)}{\mathrm {d} t}\right), \Pi_ {1} ^ {d ^ {2}} \left(\boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0}\right) \right\rangle_ {\mathcal {V} ^ {- 1}} \\ = 2 \left\langle \Pi_ {1} ^ {d ^ {2}} \left(\boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {W} (t))\right), \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\rangle_ {\mathcal {V} ^ {- 1}} \\ \leq 2 \left\langle \Pi_ {1} ^ {d ^ {2}} \left(\boldsymbol {J} \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}\right) \left[ \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right]\right), \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\rangle_ {\mathcal {V} ^ {- 1}} \\ + 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) - \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} ] \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \\ = 2 \left\langle \Pi_ {1} ^ {d ^ {2}} \left(\boldsymbol {J} \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}\right) \left[ \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) + \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \right]\right), \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\rangle_ {\mathcal {V} ^ {- 1}} \\ + 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) - \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} ] \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \\ = 2 \left\langle \Pi_ {1} ^ {d ^ {2}} \left(\boldsymbol {J} \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}\right) [ \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) ]\right), \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\rangle_ {\mathcal {V} ^ {- 1}} + 2 \| \boldsymbol {J} \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}\right) [ \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) ] \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \\ + 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {g} \left(\boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0}\right) - \boldsymbol {J} \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}\right) [ \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} ] \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

For the first term  $\left\langle \Pi_1^{d^2}(\boldsymbol {J}(\boldsymbol {W}_0)[\boldsymbol {W}_1(t)]),\boldsymbol {W}_1(t)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{-1}}$  , we know  $W_{1}(t)\in \mathcal{T}$  , and  $\mathcal{T}$  is an invariant space of  $J(W_0)$  . Recall  $J(\pmb {W}_0)|_{\mathcal{T}}[\cdot ] = \mathcal{V}\left(\Sigma \mathcal{V}^{-1}(\cdot)\right)$  , we have

$$
2 \left\langle \Pi_ {1} ^ {d ^ {2}} \left(\boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) ]\right), \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\rangle_ {\mathcal {V} ^ {- 1}} = 2 \left\langle \Sigma \mathcal {V} ^ {- 1} \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t)\right), \mathcal {V} ^ {- 1} \left(\boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t)\right) \right\rangle \leq - 2 \mu_ {1} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1}.
$$

For the second term  $2\beta \left\| \boldsymbol{J}(\boldsymbol{W}_0)[\boldsymbol{W}_2(t)]\right\|_{\mathcal{V}}\left\| \boldsymbol{W}_1(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{V}}$  we have

$$
2 \left\| \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) ] \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \leq 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) \right\| _ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} = 2 \rho \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}}.
$$

For the third term 2  $\| g(\pmb {W}(t) - \pmb {W}_0) - \pmb {J}(\pmb {W}_0)[\pmb {W}(t) - \pmb {W}_0]\|_{\mathcal{V}}\| \pmb {W}_1(t)\|_{\mathcal{V}},$  we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {g} (\boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) - \boldsymbol {J} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0}) [ \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} ] \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \leq 2 \beta \left\| \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \leq 4 \beta \left(\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {F} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right) \\ \leq 4 \beta \left(K ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus we have shown the following. Note so far we have not used the assumption that  $\mathbf{W}$  is rank-  $k$ .

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2}}{\mathrm {d} t} \leq - 2 \mu_ {1} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2} + 2 \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \left(\rho \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} + 2 \beta K ^ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2} + 2 \beta \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right),
$$

that is,

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \log \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2}}{\mathrm {d} t} \leq - 2 \mu_ {1} + 4 \beta K ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} + \frac {4 \beta \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + 2 \rho \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}}}{\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}}}. \tag {43}
$$

Let  $T \coloneqq \sup \{t \geq 0 : \| \pmb{W}_1(t) \|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq \frac{m}{2K}\}$ . Setting  $\pmb{W}' = \pmb{W}_0$  in Lemma K.2, we have for  $t < T$ ,  $r = \| \pmb{W}(t) - \pmb{W}_0 \|_{\mathrm{F},1} \leq \| \pmb{W}(t) - \pmb{W}_0 \|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq K \| \pmb{W}(t) - \pmb{W}_0 \|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq \frac{m}{2}$ . Thus,

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} = \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \leq \frac {5 \left\| \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F} , 1} ^ {2}}{m} \leq \frac {5 K ^ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2}}{m} \leq \frac {5}{4} m.
$$

Thus, from (43) we can derive that

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d} \log \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2}}{\mathrm {d} t} \leq - 2 \mu_ {1} + K ^ {2} (2 9 \beta + 1 0 \rho / m)) \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \leq - \mu_ {1}. \tag {44}
$$

Since  $\mu_1 < 0$ ,  $\| \pmb{W}_1(t) \|_{\mathcal{V}}$  decreases for  $[0, T)$ . Thus  $T$  must be  $\infty$ , otherwise  $\| \pmb{W}_1(T) \|_{\mathcal{V}} = \lim_{t \to T^-} \| \pmb{W}_1(t) \|_{\mathcal{V}} < R_1$ . Contradiction.

Therefore, for any  $t \in [0, \infty)$ , we have  $\| W_1(t) \|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq \| W_1(0) \|_{\mathcal{V}} e^{-\frac{\mu_1}{2} t}$ . That is,

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {\infty} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} d t \leq \frac {2}{\mu_ {1}} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (0) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} \leq \frac {2 R}{\mu_ {1}}.
$$

Thus from (44), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {W} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} = \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \leq \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (0) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \exp \left(- \mu_ {1} t + \frac {K ^ {2}}{2} (2 9 \beta + 1 0 \rho / m) \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} d t\right) \\ \leq \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (0) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \exp \left(- \mu_ {1} t + \frac {K ^ {2} R}{\mu_ {1}} (2 9 \beta + 1 0 \rho / m)\right) \\ =: C \left\| \boldsymbol {W} (0) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} e ^ {- \mu_ {1} t}, \\ \end{array}
$$

which completes the proof.

![](images/5d91205e07eda87c27b7ad01b4b3d5a4cdb2b552853b403e5f0a7562ee79efa4.jpg)

# K.2 ALMOST RANK- $k$  INITIALIZATION

We use  $M(t)$  to denote the top- $k$  components of  $W(t)$  in SVD, and  $N(t)$  to denote the rest part, i.e.,  $W(t) - M(t)$ . One can think  $M(t)$  as the main part and  $N(t)$  as the negligible part.

Below we show that for deep overparametrized matrix factorization, where  $\mathbf{W}(t)$  satisfies (42), if the trajectory is initialized at some  $\mathbf{W}(0)$  in a small neighborhood of the  $k$ -th critical point  $\mathbf{W}_0$  of deep GLRL, and  $\mathbf{W}(0)$  is approximately rank-  $k$ , in the sense that  $\mathbf{N}(0)$  is very small, then  $\inf_{t\geq 0}\| \mathbf{W}(t) - \mathbf{W}_0\|_{\mathcal{V}}$  is roughly at the same magnitude of  $N(0)$ .

Theorem K.4 (Linear convergence of almost rank-  $k$  matrices, deep case). Suppose  $\mathbf{W}_0$  is a critical point of rank  $k$  and  $\mathbf{W}_0$  satisfies Assumption K.1, there exists constants  $C_0$  and  $r$ , such that if  $C_0\| \mathbf{N}(0)\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq \| \mathbf{W}_1(0)\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq r$ , then there exists a time  $T$  and constants  $C,C^{\prime}$ , such that

(1).  $\| \pmb {W}(t) - \pmb {W}_0\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq Ce^{-\mu_1t / 2}\| \pmb {W}(0) - \pmb {W}_0\|_{\mathcal{V}},$  for  $t\le T$  
(2).  $\| \pmb {W}(T) - \pmb{W}_0\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq C'\| \pmb {N}(0)\|_{\mathrm{F}}$

Proof. When  $\| \pmb{W}(t) - \pmb{W}_0\|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq \frac{\lambda_{\min}(\pmb{W}_0)}{4}$ ,  $\| \pmb{N}(t)\|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq \frac{\lambda_{\min}(\pmb{W}_0)}{4}$ , thus we have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {M} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {W} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1} + \left\| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1} \leq \frac {\lambda_ {\min} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0})}{2},
$$

thus by Lemma K.2, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \leq \| \boldsymbol {M} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} + \| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \\ \leq \frac {5 \| \boldsymbol {M} (t) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \| _ {\mathrm {F} , 1} ^ {2}}{\lambda_ {\min } (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0})} + \| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \\ \leq \frac {1 0 \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F} , 1} ^ {2} + 1 0 \| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}}{\lambda_ {\min} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0})} + \| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \\ \leq \frac {1 0 K ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} ^ {2} + 1 0 \| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}}{\lambda_ {\operatorname* {m i n}} (\boldsymbol {W} _ {0})} + \| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus we can pick constant  $C_0$  large enough and  $r$  small enough, such that for any  $t \geq 0$ , if  $C_0\| N(t)\|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq \| W_1(t)\|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq r$ , then it holds that:

- The "small terms" in the RHS of (43) satisfies that

$$
4 \beta K ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} + \frac {4 \beta \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + 2 \rho \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {2} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}}}{\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}}} \leq C _ {1} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (t) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} + C _ {2} \| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \mu_ {1}
$$

for some  $C_1$  and  $C_2$  independent of  $t$ .

- The spectral norm  $\frac{1}{2}\| \nabla f(\pmb {W}(t))\| _2\leq \| \nabla f(\pmb {W}_0)\| _2\eqqcolon \rho$  for all  $t\geq 0$  
$\forall x <   r,\frac{\kappa_Lx^{\frac{L}{2} - 1}}{(L - 2)\rho} >\frac{2}{\mu_1}\ln \frac{2r}{C_0x},$  where  $\kappa_{L} = 1 - 0.5^{\frac{L - 2}{L}}.$

Note these conditions can always be satisfied by some  $C_0$  and  $r$  because we can first find 3 groups  $(C_0, r)$  to satisfy each individual condition, and then take the maximal  $C_0$  and minimal  $r$ , it's easy to check these conditions are still verified. And we let  $T_{C_0,r}$  be the earliest time that such condition, i.e.,  $C_0\| \boldsymbol{N}(t)\|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq \| \boldsymbol{W}_1(t)\|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq r$  fails. Thus by (43), for  $t \in [0,T_{C_0,r})$ , we have  $\| \boldsymbol{W}(t)\|_{\mathcal{V}} = \| \boldsymbol{W}_1(t)\|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq \| \boldsymbol{W}_1(0)\|_{\mathcal{V}} e^{-\frac{\mu_1t}{2}} = \| \boldsymbol{W}(0)\|_{\mathcal{V}} e^{-\frac{\mu_1t}{2}}$ . Thus (1) holds for any  $T$  smaller than  $T_{C_0,r}$ . If  $T_{C_0,r} = \infty$ , then clearly we can pick a sufficiently large  $T$ , such that (2) holds. Therefore, below it suffices to consider the case where  $T_{C_0,r}$  is finite. And we know the condition that fails must be  $C_0\| \boldsymbol{N}(t)\|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq \| \boldsymbol{W}_1(t)\|_{\mathcal{V}}$ , i.e.  $C_0\| \boldsymbol{N}(T_{C_0,r})\|_{\mathrm{F}} = \| \boldsymbol{W}_1(T_{C_0,r})\|_{\mathcal{V}}$ .

By (35) in Lemma I.7, we have

$$
\left| \| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {2}{L} - 1} - \| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {2}{L} - 1} \right| \leq (L - 2) \rho t.
$$

Define  $T' := \frac{\kappa_L \|N(0)\|_2^{\frac{2}{L} - 1}}{(L - 2)\rho}$ , we know for any  $t < T'$ , we have  $\left\| N(0)\right\|_2^{\frac{2}{L} - 1} - \left\| N(t)\right\|_2^{\frac{2}{L} - 1} \leq \kappa_L\|N(t)\|_2^{\frac{2}{L} - 1}$ . That is,

$$
\frac {\| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {L}{2} - 1}}{\| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {2} ^ {\frac {2}{L} - 1}} \in \left[ 1 - \kappa_ {L}, \frac {1}{1 - \kappa_ {L}} \right] = \left[ 0. 5 ^ {\frac {L - 2}{L}}, 0. 5 ^ {- \frac {L - 2}{L}} \right] \Longrightarrow \frac {\| \boldsymbol {N} (t) \| _ {2}}{\| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {2}} \in [ 1 / 2, 2 ].
$$

Now we claim it must hold that  $T' \geq T_{C_0,r}$ . Otherwise, we have

$$
\frac {C _ {0}}{2} \| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {2} \leq C _ {0} \| \boldsymbol {N} (T ^ {\prime}) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (T ^ {\prime}) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \leq e ^ {- \mu_ {1} T ^ {\prime} / 2} \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (0) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \leq e ^ {- \mu_ {1} T ^ {\prime} / 2} r.
$$

Therefore,  $\frac{\kappa_L\|N(0)\|_2^{\frac{2}{L} - 1}}{(L - 2)\rho} = T' \leq \frac{2}{\mu_1}\ln \frac{2r}{C_0\|N(0)\|_2}$ , which contradicts to the definition of  $C_0$  and  $r$ .

As a result, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 C _ {0} \sqrt {d} \| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {2} \geq 2 C _ {0} \| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \geq C _ {0} \| \boldsymbol {N} \left(T _ {c _ {0}, r}\right) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} = \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} \left(T _ {C _ {0}, r}\right) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} \\ \geq \| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (0) \| _ {\mathcal {V}} e ^ {- \mu_ {1} T _ {C _ {0, r} / 2}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

and therefore,

$$
T _ {C _ {0}, r} \leq \frac {2}{\mu_ {1}} \ln \frac {\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} (0) \| _ {\mathcal {V}}}{2 \sqrt {d} C _ {0} \| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {\mathrm {F}}}.
$$

Thus by Lemma K.2, we know

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {W} \left(T _ {C _ {0}, r}\right) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {W} \left(T _ {C _ {0}, r}\right) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 1} + \left\| \boldsymbol {W} \left(T _ {C _ {0}, r}\right) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \\ \leq K \left\| \boldsymbol {W} _ {1} \left(T _ {C _ {0}, r}\right) \right\| _ {\mathcal {V}} + \left\| \boldsymbol {M} \left(T _ {C _ {0}, r}\right) - \boldsymbol {W} _ {0} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} + \left\| \boldsymbol {N} \left(T _ {C _ {0}, r}\right) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}, 2} \\ \leq O (\| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {\mathrm {F}}) + O (\| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}) + O (\| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \| _ {\mathrm {F}}) \\ = O \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {N} (0) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

# K.3 PROOF FOR THEOREM 6.4

Proof for Theorem 6.4. Let  $C_0, r$  be the constants predicted by Theorem K.4 w.r.t. to  $\overline{W}(\infty)$ . We claim that we can pick large enough constant  $T$ , and  $\alpha_0$  sufficiently small, such that for all  $\alpha \leq \alpha_0$ , the initial condition in Theorem K.4 holds, i.e.  $C_0 \| N(0) \|_{\mathbb{F}} \leq \| W_1(0) \|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq r$ , where  $W(0) := \phi\left(\alpha I, \frac{\alpha^{-(P-1)}}{2\mu_1^{-(P-1)}} + T\right)$ .

This is because we can first ensure  $\left\| \overline{W}(T) - \overline{W}(\infty) \right\|_2$  is sufficiently small, i.e., smaller than  $\frac{r}{2}$ . By Theorem 6.2, we know when  $\alpha \to 0$ ,  $\left\| \overline{W}(T) - W(0) \right\|_{\mathcal{V}} \leq K \left\| \overline{W}(T) - W(0) \right\|_{\mathrm{F}} = o(1)$  and  $\| N(0) \|_{\mathrm{F}} = O(\alpha)$ .

By Theorem K.4, we know there is a time  $T$  (either  $T_{C_0,r}$  or some sufficiently large number when  $T_{C_0,r} = \infty$ ), such that  $\| \boldsymbol{W}(T) - \boldsymbol{W}_0 \|_{\mathrm{F}} = O(\| \boldsymbol{N}(0) \|_{\mathrm{F}}) = O(\alpha)$ .
